🎓 Capital gains tax on agricultural land sale

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Capital Gains Tax on Agricultural Land Sale in India — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Rural agricultural land is NOT a capital asset under Section 2(14)(iii) — its sale attracts no capital gains tax at all.
  • The rural test is mechanical: the nearest municipality’s census population, and aerial distance of 2 / 6 / 8 km.
  • Urban agricultural land is taxed like any property; long-term starts at 24 months.
  • Section 54B exemption needs TWO years of actual farming before sale, reinvestment in farmland within two years, and a three-year lock-in on the new land.
  • Section 50C lets the taxman substitute the stamp-duty value when you sell below it.

When the tax applies at all

One question decides everything: is the land RURAL or URBAN under the Income Tax Act? Rural agricultural land is excluded from the very definition of a capital asset by Section 2(14)(iii) — no capital asset, no capital gain, nothing to tax. Urban agricultural land gets no carve-out and is taxed like any other asset. Two owners can sell what looks like the same plot and one pays nothing while the other owes tax on the whole gain — purely because of where the land sits.

The rural test — mechanical, not agricultural

Nothing to do with how the land is farmed; everything to do with location and population. Land is RURAL if it lies outside a municipality or cantonment of 10,000+ people, AND outside these aerial distances from those limits: 2 km where the population is 10,000–1 lakh; 6 km where it is 1–10 lakh; 8 km where it exceeds 10 lakh. Population comes from the last published census; distance is measured AERIALLY — as the crow flies, not as the road winds. Inside the limits = urban agricultural land = taxable. One nuance: the exemption attaches to the LAND — a farmhouse, well or standing trees sold in the same deal are valued and treated separately.

Example: Two farmers sell near Hyderabad (population >10 lakh, so the 8 km band applies). One’s land sits 9 km aerially from the municipal limit — rural, gain fully exempt. The other’s sits 7 km out — urban, fully taxable. Same crop, same buyer profile, two kilometres of geometry worth lakhs in tax.

The second test: was it actually farmed?

Classification decides the tax; CULTIVATION decides whether your claim survives scrutiny. Revenue records carry seasonal cultivation entries — the evidence that agricultural operations actually happened — and Section 54B leans on exactly that record for its two-year farming condition. Land held bare as pure investment is where exemption claims die at assessment.

How the gain is computed

Sale price minus purchase cost and expenses. Held 24+ months: long-term. The current long-term regime taxes gains at 12.5% without indexation (indexation having been withdrawn for post-July-2024 sales). And Section 50C looms over the price itself: sell below the stamp-duty value and the department can treat THAT value as your deemed sale price, subject to a tolerance margin — the guideline value quietly setting your tax floor.

Rural vs urban at a glance

AspectRural agricultural landUrban agricultural land
Capital asset?No — s.2(14)(iii) excludes itYes
Capital gains taxNoneLTCG/STCG as applicable
TDS on sale (194-IA)Expressly excluded1% at ₹50 lakh+ (resident seller)
Exemption routesNot needed54B (farmland), 54EC (bonds)

The exits: 54B and 54EC

Section 54B — individuals and HUFs: reinvest the gain in NEW agricultural land within two years, provided the sold land was farmed for the two years before sale; money not yet reinvested by the return-filing date parks in a Capital Gains Account Scheme; the new land locks in for three years — sell early and the exemption unwinds. Section 54EC — the alternative for long-term gains: NHAI/REC/PFC/IRFC bonds within six months (₹50 lakh cap, five-year lock).

The taxes that ride alongside

TDS: buyers deduct 1% under 194-IA on urban agricultural land at ₹50 lakh+ — rural land is expressly outside the section. NRI sellers: Section 195 instead, at much higher rates — plan with a lower-deduction certificate. 50C: as above, the stamp-value floor.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is rural agricultural land exempt? Fully — it is not a capital asset. How is “rural” defined? The census-population + aerial-distance test above. Indexation still available? Not under the current 12.5% regime. Urban land exemptions? 54B with the farming and lock-in conditions; 54EC bonds. TDS on farmland? Urban yes (₹50L+), rural no. Don’t want to buy farmland again? 54EC bonds, or pay the 12.5% and stay liquid.

Bottom line

Before selling any farmland, establish three things in writing: the aerial distance to the nearest 10,000+ municipality, the census figure that sets the band, and the cultivation record. Those three lines decide whether the tax is zero, exempt-with-conditions, or fully payable — get a CA’s letter where kilometres decide lakhs.

For you: For your JD landowner partners this is THE conversation: many Ranga Reddy parcels sit inside Hyderabad’s 8 km band — urban, taxable — while one village further out is exempt. Knowing which side of the line a family’s land falls (and flagging 54B’s two-year farming condition early) makes you the developer who saved them lakhs before the deal even signed. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 How to calculate stamp duty and registration charges on land

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: How to Calculate Stamp Duty and Registration Charges on Land — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Stamp duty is a STATE tax on the transfer; registration charges are a separate fee for recording it.
  • Both are generally payable before the sale deed can register.
  • Rates vary by state, property type, buyer category and value — property is a state subject.
  • The base is generally the HIGHER of the sale price and the guideline/stamp-duty value.
  • Online calculators estimate the bill in advance — with current-year rates only.

The two levies, separated

Stamp duty: a statutory tax on the property transaction itself — the state’s cut for recognising the transfer. Registration charges: the fee for recording the deed in official records. Different levies, different lines on the challan; both generally must be paid before the Sub-Registrar registers your deed. Budget them together: in Telangana the combined package on a sale deed runs about 7.5% of the value.

Understanding stamp duty

Each state’s own stamp law governs — which is why rates, concessions and even calculation methods differ across borders. The two planning variables: the STATE-SPECIFIC RATE (the biggest number in your closing costs), and POTENTIAL EXEMPTIONS — many states discount duty for women buyers or first-time purchasers. Verify the current rate and valuation rules before finalising any purchase; budgets built on last year’s percentages miss.

Registration charges explained

Payable for entering the sale deed into the official record — the act that makes your ownership publicly traceable and your deed retrievable as a certified copy forever. Rates vary by state and can carry minimum or maximum limits (some states cap registration fees in absolute rupees; others charge a flat percentage — Telangana’s is 0.5%).

The calculation, step by step

The maths is simple once the base is fixed. 1) Determine the transaction value — the actual agreed price. 2) Check the guideline / stamp-duty value for the survey number. 3) The BASE is the higher of the two. 4) Apply the state’s duty rate. 5) Add registration charges. 6) Add any state surcharge or cess. The declared price never beats the guideline floor: under-declare and the department simply computes on its own benchmark.

Example: Telangana example: plot agreed at ₹80 lakh, guideline value ₹90 lakh. Base = ₹90 lakh. At ~7.5% combined, the SRO bill is ≈ ₹6.75 lakh — on the guideline figure, not the price. A buyer who budgeted 7.5% of ₹80 lakh is ₹75,000 short at the counter.

Using a calculator effectively

State portals and property sites host duty calculators: feed location and value, get the estimate instantly. Two disciplines: confirm the calculator uses THIS year’s rates (states tweak percentages in budget sessions), and use it comparatively — running the same investment across district or state lines can reveal lakhs of difference in friction cost alone.

Exemptions and reductions

State-dependent concessions exist for women purchasers, affordable-housing projects, specified FAMILY transfers (gift/settlement deeds between close relatives often carry nominal duty), and other notified categories. Always check eligibility before registration — concessions are claimed at the counter, not refunded after.

Why rates vary regionally

Property is a state subject: each government prescribes its own rates and concessions, tuned to its revenue needs, property type (commercial vs residential) and urban demand. The practical rule: never carry one state’s percentage in your head into another state’s deal — verify with the registration department that will actually stamp your deed.

Key takeaways for buyers

☐ Calculate duty BEFORE signing the agreement.
☐ Verify the guideline value for the exact survey number.
☐ Put registration charges in the budget line, not the surprise line.
☐ Check concession eligibility (women buyers, family transfers).
☐ Verify ownership documents before registration — duty paid on a bad title is money on a bad title.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Calculated on sale price or guideline value? The higher of the two. Same across states? No — rates, fees and concessions all differ. First-time buyer discounts? In some states; check the current notification. Documents needed to pay and register? The deed, identity/PAN, the challan or e-stamp, and the property’s record extracts. Why estimate in advance? Because 6–8% of a land deal is lakhs — a closing-day shortfall can cost you the slot and, with a hard deadline in the agreement, the advance.

Bottom line

Duty and registration are the certain, calculable part of a land deal — the only closing surprise they ever produce is the one a five-minute advance calculation would have removed.

For you: Quote plot prices to your buyers as “all-in at registration” maths — price + ~7.5% + mutation — in the first meeting. Transparency about the state’s cut builds the trust that closes files. On your own acquisitions, model duty into land cost per acre from day one: on a ₹10 Cr assembly it is ₹75 lakh of real money, not a rounding error. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 Guideline value vs market value: what every land buyer should know

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Guideline Value vs Market Value: What Every Land Buyer Should Know — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Stamp duty is charged on the HIGHER of the guideline value and the price you pay.
  • Guideline value usually lags the market — but in stagnant pockets it can sit ABOVE what land is worth.
  • The Income Tax Act calls every state’s version the “stamp duty value”, whatever its local name.
  • Buying BELOW the benchmark can create taxable income in your hands under Section 56(2)(x).
  • A bank-transfer token on the agreement date can LOCK the benchmark at that date — cash cannot.
  • Market value for land runs on registered comparables, frontage, road access and zone.

The two numbers, defined

Guideline value (circle rate, ready-reckoner rate, unit rate — the name varies by state): the registration department’s benchmark valuation for each locality and land type, existing to stop parties under-declaring prices to dodge duty. The Income Tax Act sweeps all the local names into one term: the stamp duty value. Market value: what a willing buyer actually pays a willing seller — set by demand, not by notification.

Duty runs on the higher number

Duty is computed on the guideline value or the declared price, WHICHEVER IS HIGHER. Declaring a lower price does not help — where the declared figure falls below the benchmark, the department simply applies the benchmark. Exact rates vary by state; calculate with your jurisdiction’s numbers.

The trap: buying below the guideline value

Buying ABOVE the benchmark costs you duty; buying BELOW it can cost you INCOME TAX. Under Section 56(2)(x), where you buy immovable property for less than its stamp-duty value, the shortfall is treated as YOUR income under “Income from Other Sources” (subject to the tolerance band). A genuine bargain thus gets taxed as if the discount were a gift to you. One protective mechanism: where agreement and registration dates differ, the benchmark ON THE AGREEMENT DATE can be adopted — but only if part of the consideration moved by cheque, draft or electronic transfer on or before that date. A CASH token does not qualify. And states revise values periodically, so always verify the figure on the date that matters to YOUR transaction.

Example: You agree a plot at ₹60 lakh in March by handshake; the state revises the guideline to ₹75 lakh in April; you register in June. Duty computes on ₹75 lakh — and 56(2)(x) can tax ₹15 lakh as your income. Had you paid even a ₹1 lakh token by BANK TRANSFER with a written agreement in March, the March benchmark could have locked. The transfer mode of one token payment carried lakhs of consequence.

What moves the guideline value

Location does most of the work — established and fast-developing pockets carry higher benchmarks because demand is already on record. Then: zoning — what the land may legally be used for feeds the assessed band; infrastructure — a new road or transit line lifts the benchmark, but only at the NEXT revision; and revision frequency itself varies by state, which is why benchmarks drift from reality in both directions.

What determines market value

For land the drivers are physical and legal, not cosmetic: frontage and road width; corner position; whether the survey number matches the ground; the master-plan zone and whether conversion is complete; and above all, what comparable parcels nearby actually REGISTERED for — a different number from what they were advertised at.

The two values compared

AspectGuideline valueMarket value
Fixed byState registration departmentBuyers and sellers, deal by deal
DecidesDuty floor, TDS thresholds, 56(2)(x)/50C exposureThe price you negotiate
MovesAt periodic state revisionsContinuously, with demand
EvidencePublished departmental tablesRegistered comparable sales
Typical relationshipLags the marketLeads — except in stagnant pockets

When to rely on which number

Use the GUIDELINE value to budget duty and to check where your price sits against the tolerance band. Use MARKET value to negotiate and to judge whether a price is defensible. And note the third opinion in the room: a lender’s own valuation — where it lands well below the asking price, confirm the bank’s figure before you commit; banks are paid to be unromantic.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is duty on guideline or market value? The higher of guideline and declared price. What if I buy below guideline? Duty on the benchmark anyway — plus possible 56(2)(x) income in your hands. Is guideline always lower than market? Usually, not always — stagnant pockets invert it, and there you pay duty on more than the land is worth. How do I find it? The state registration portal, by village/survey number (Telangana: the IGRS unit-rate search). Negotiate on which? Market value — built from registered comparables; the guideline only tells you the state’s floor.

Bottom line

Two numbers, two jobs: the state’s benchmark sets the floor for duty and tax; registered comparables set the truth for price. The gap between them decides duty, TDS and tax exposure — verify the benchmark with the registration department before making any offer, and take professional advice where price and benchmark diverge sharply.

For you: You live in this gap professionally. Rising guideline values in your belts raise buyers’ closing costs — get grandfathering by locking agreements with BANK-transferred tokens before revision dates. And watch revisions as market intelligence: the state raising a village’s benchmark is the state telling you where it sees demand going. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 Common land scams in India and how to protect yourself

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Common Land Scams in India and How to Protect Yourself — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Fraud thrives where records are scattered: ~95% of RoRs are computerised, but only ~68% of cadastral maps.
  • The deadliest scam is the LEGAL-LOOKING one: registered deeds on assigned land are void — registration proves nothing.
  • Seven patterns cover most of the market: forged titles, restricted land, duplicate sales, PoA abuse, encroachment, benami, fake pre-launch schemes.
  • The defence is always the same: pull the records YOURSELF — RoR, EC, prohibited list, title chain.
  • Remote buyers and absentee owners are the favourite targets.

Why India’s land market attracts so much fraud

Fraud grows wherever information is scarce and checking is hard. India’s records are digitising UNEVENLY — about 95% of Records of Rights computerised but only 68% of cadastral maps, with some districts still on ageing paper — and every state runs its own system. The favourite victims: buyers unfamiliar with local revenue records, people purchasing remotely, and plots whose owners are absent or unaware something is being sold in their name.

The most devastating one: restricted land sold as normal

Certain land legally CANNOT be freely sold: government-assigned land, tribal land, ceiling-surplus land, endowment (temple) land, forest land. The scam: a legitimate-looking registered document, payment made, registration completed — and years or decades later the government reclaims the land because it was assigned all along. Buyer loses land, money and everything built on it. The legal trap: the prohibited-list mechanism (Section 22-A) only BLOCKS registration of parcels actually on the Collector’s list — unlisted assigned parcels register fine — but the transfer-ban law voids the sale regardless of what any registrar accepted. So: check the prohibited register AND the revenue record’s remarks, always.

Seven scams to know

1) Title fraud and forged documents — the most widespread: a fraudster forges papers, poses as owner or PoA holder, sells land that is not theirs; forged paper carries zero legal weight, which is why the CHAIN matters more than the current deed. 2) Restricted-land sales — as above. 3) Duplicate sales — one plot sold to two or more buyers, exploiting slow registration; not a pure first-to-register race (a deed relates back to its execution date under Section 47), but the genuinely prior sale usually prevails and everyone else litigates. 4) PoA abuse — forged, expired or revoked PoAs used to sell without the owner’s knowledge; an unregistered PoA, or one not clearly authorising sale, is a warning. 5) Encroachment folded in — the boundary shown quietly includes government or neighbouring land; good title to part, nothing on the rest; a boundary survey catches it. 6) Benami deals — property in someone else’s name to hide the real buyer; illegal under the 2016 Benami law, with confiscation and up to seven years’ rigorous imprisonment. 7) Fake pre-launch schemes — units sold in unapproved projects with assured-return promises backed by post-dated cheques that bounce; RERA bars selling before registration, so a pre-launch pitch WITHOUT a RERA number is the tell.

Definition — Benami transaction: a purchase held in a name-lender’s name while someone else pays and controls it. Since the 2016 amendment the property can be confiscated outright — buyer-side participation is itself the crime.

Red flags and what they mean

Warning signWhat it usually meansWhat to do
Price well below marketDefect the seller knows and you don’tSlow down; the discount IS the disclosure
Pressure to close fastVerification window being shortened deliberatelyNever pay before checks complete
Seller supplies all documents, resists your own EC pullPapers may be curated or forgedPull EC and RoR yourself from official sources
Sale via PoA, owner unreachablePossible PoA abuse or a dead/unaware principalMeet or video-verify the owner; check PoA registration
“Registration will fix everything”Confusing registration with valid titleRemember: void transfers register too
No RERA number on a project pitchUnapproved / pre-launch schemeVerify on the state RERA portal before any token

Documents to check before any purchase

☐ Title + sale deed — seller’s name matches the record; chain traced 30 years.
☐ Record of Rights — owner, classification, mutation history, restriction remarks.
☐ EC — pulled by YOU from the sub-registrar; never the seller’s copy.
☐ Land use / conversion — classification confirmed; conversion completed if it was agricultural.
☐ Mutation — updated, no restriction notes.
☐ Tax receipts — dues attach to the LAND, not the old owner.
☐ RERA — verified on the portal for any plotted or builder project.
☐ PoA, if involved — authorises sale, registered where required, traceable to the real owner.

What maps can and cannot catch

Survey-number maps, master-plan overlays and satellite imagery catch boundary and zoning mismatches — whether the parcel’s ground reality matches the deed, and whether it sits in a restricted zone — before a site visit. They cannot read a title chain, tell a forged deed from a genuine one, or check the prohibited list. That work stays with the sub-registrar’s records and a revenue-law advocate.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Most common scam? Title fraud with forged documents. How do I know if land has transfer restrictions? RoR remarks + the state’s prohibited-property register. Can I buy through a PoA? Yes, with independent verification of the PoA and the principal — PoA sales are not inherently illegitimate. What if I unknowingly bought restricted land? The transfer is void; get a revenue advocate immediately. Is RERA-registered land always safe? Safer — but RERA checks the project, not the title chain; do both. How to report fraud? Police complaint (EOW for large frauds) + civil suit; registration of a case does not undo the transfer, so prevention is the real remedy.

Bottom line

Nearly every land scam succeeds at the same moment: when the buyer accepts someone else’s paperwork instead of pulling their own. RoR, EC, prohibited list, 30-year chain, boundary survey — five self-service checks defeat all seven scams.

For you: Your buyers fear exactly this list — which makes it your marketing. A venture that publishes its own verification file (chain, EC, prohibited-list clearance, DTCP approval) sells trust the unorganised market cannot. Internally: rule — no landowner conversation advances until the RoR and prohibited-list check are done, however warm the introduction. 🎓 Assigned land: why it cannot be sold and what the law says

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Assigned Land in India: Why It Cannot Be Sold and What the Law Says — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Assigned land = government surplus/wasteland granted to landless poor families for cultivation or house sites — possession and use, NOT free ownership.
  • Transfers are prohibited or void under state land-reform laws; a registered sale deed changes nothing.
  • The government can RESUME the land at any time, with no compensation to the buyer — and buying it can carry up to six months’ jail.
  • Narrow exceptions: inheritance by heirs, bank mortgages, and good-faith pre-Act purchases by landless poor persons.
  • The whole defence is two checks: the revenue record’s remarks and the Section 22-A prohibited list.

What assigned land is

Government surplus or wasteland handed to landless poor persons — typically agricultural labourers or SC/ST families — for cultivation or as a house site, under land-reform and welfare schemes. What transfers is possession and limited use rights; ownership stays CONDITIONAL with the state. The condition that matters to every buyer: the grant usually prohibits sale.

Why the law bans its sale

The point of assignment is welfare — land in the hands of the landless. If assignees could sell, the poor would be bought out for quick cash and the policy would defeat itself; so state laws (in Telangana, the Assigned Lands (Prohibition of Transfers) Act) bar selling, gifting, leasing or otherwise transferring without government permission, and declare violating transfers invalid.

What “void ab initio” means

Treated as if the transaction never legally happened — no title passes, ever. Even land that changed hands TEN times through ten registered deeds: every link is equally invalid. A registered document does not make you the owner here; the paper has no legal weight at all. One narrow protection exists: a landless poor person who bought in good faith, for value, from the original assignee BEFORE the Act commenced and was in possession on that date — it protects the poor, not an investor.

Definition — Void ab initio: void “from the beginning” — not cancelled later, but never valid at any moment. Nothing downstream of it can be valid either.

What happens if it is sold anyway

The government can resume the land at ANY time — no limitation period runs in its favour going away — and reassign it to another eligible landless family. The buyer gets no compensation; no civil court can decree the sale into existence. And the consequence sellers never mention: acquiring assigned land in breach of the prohibition is an OFFENCE carrying up to six months’ imprisonment. The buyer is not just a victim in this law’s eyes.

Transferability by land category

CategoryFreely transferable?Notes
Patta (private) landYesOrdinary sale deed + registration
Assigned landNoTransfer void; resumption + penal risk
Tribal-area landHighly restrictedTransfers to non-tribals barred in scheduled areas
Ceiling-surplus landNoVested in government for redistribution
Endowment (temple) landNoAlienation needs statutory sanction, rarely given
Forest landNoCentral law bars de-reservation without approval

What an assignee CAN do

Cultivate for the family; pass the land to legal heirs (inheritance is generally not a prohibited transfer); mortgage it to a scheduled bank, co-operative or notified institution — the one security the Act permits; build a house where the assignment was a house site. And in some states, apply to the Collector for DE-RESTRICTION — converting it into regular patta land eligible for sale once eligibility conditions are met. Rules vary; the revenue authority’s word is the only one that counts.

How to check whether a plot is assigned

Pull the latest Record of Rights from the state revenue department and read the REMARKS column for “assigned”, “granted” or restriction notes. Check the state’s prohibited-property register (Section 22-A list) for the survey number. Ask the local revenue office directly. And read the original ASSIGNMENT ORDER — it states plainly whether transfer is prohibited. Fifteen minutes of record-reading is the difference between owning land and owning a legal dead end.

Example: The classic Hyderabad-belt trap: a corridor plot at 40% below market, seller waving a registered deed from a 2009 purchase. The RoR remarks column reads “asgd”. That deed — and the one before it — are both void; the discount was the disclosure. Walk away; no lawyer can cure it.

If you are the assignee wanting to sell

Apply to the competent revenue authority for removal of the transfer restriction where state law permits, BEFORE entering any agreement. For SC/ST or tribal-origin land, confirm whether Collector approval is mandatory. Selling first and regularising later is not a sequence the law recognises.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Can assigned land be sold after N years? No automatic ripening — only formal de-restriction changes its status. Why do registered deeds exist on assigned land? Because registration only blocks parcels actually on the prohibited list; the registrar’s stamp never validates a void transfer. Can family inherit it? Yes — heirs succeed; that is not a prohibited transfer. Can it be mortgaged? Only to banks/institutions the Act permits. Can it ever become freehold? In some states, via Collector-approved conversion to patta land.

Bottom line

Assigned land is the one category where late homework has NO remedy — no cure, no compensation, no sympathetic court. The RoR remarks and the 22-A list, checked before any money moves, are the entire safeguard.

For you: For a plotting developer this is the first gate on every parcel — before price, before layout dreams: Dharani/RoR remarks + 22-A check on each survey number. One assigned survey number inside an assembled venture can poison the whole layout’s marketability. Make the check a fixed line item in your acquisition template. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 How to resolve land boundary disputes using survey records

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: How to Resolve Land Boundary Disputes Using Survey Records — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Most boundary fights trace to outdated surveys, vague old deeds, or inherited land split informally without fresh measurement.
  • Cadastral maps and Field Measurement Books carry more weight in these disputes than any other evidence.
  • A fresh official survey from the revenue department is the FIRST move, not the last resort.
  • Twelve years of open hostile possession can ripen into adverse possession — delay has a price in land, not just time.
  • Digitisation is real but uneven: ~95% of RoRs computerised, ~68% of maps, geo-referencing at ~49%.

Why these disputes keep happening

Outdated or inconsistent records; ambiguous boundary descriptions in older sale deeds; inherited property subdivided informally without fresh surveys; delayed mutation. Many records originate in the colonial era, and though the national modernisation drive (DILRMP) has digitised much, progress is uneven — leaving some areas running on decades-old measurements while land prices around them multiplied.

Survey records: where every resolution starts

Two records outrank everything else. The cadastral map — the official parcel map identifying each survey number and its neighbours. The Field Measurement Book (FMB) — the recorded measurements from official surveys of each parcel. Together with title documents and revenue records, these are what authorities and courts use to determine where one property legally ends and the next begins — and they beat personal recollections and “the old tamarind tree” descriptions every time.

Definition — Field Measurement Book (FMB): the survey department’s measurement sketch of a single survey number — sides, angles, extent. In a boundary fight it is the closest thing to ground truth on paper.

The resolution playbook, step by step

1) Assemble your papers — sale deed, mutation entries, tax receipts, old photographs; download the RoR from the state portal; and FIRST check that the survey number on your deed matches the Record of Rights — a mismatch there gets fixed before anything else. 2) Apply for an official government survey — a certified surveyor measures and marks the actual boundary on site; the report becomes central evidence in any later proceeding. 3) Talk first — holding a government survey report changes the conversation from your word against theirs to a neutral document; direct dialogue and state mediation services resolve more than people expect. 4) Legal notice — if the survey supports you, a notice puts your position on record and signals escalation. 5) Revenue authority or civil court — a permanent injunction (Specific Relief Act, s.38) stops ongoing encroachment; a declaratory suit (s.34) establishes ownership and fixes the line; a possession suit recovers taken land. Courts often appoint a local commissioner (CPC Order XXVI Rule 9) to measure and report — influential, though weighed with all the other evidence.

Resolution routes compared

RouteNeedsProducesTime
Official survey + negotiationSurvey fee, both parties talkingMarked boundary, often a settlementWeeks–months
MediationWillingness; state mediation servicesRecorded compromiseMonths
Revenue proceedingsSurvey report, revenue recordsAdministrative demarcation ordersMonths
Civil suit (injunction / declaration / possession)Advocate, full evidence, court feesBinding decree; commissioner’s measurementYears

What digitisation has (and has not) changed

Less than the headlines suggest. About 95% of RoRs are computerised — the TEXT record is usually a download. But cadastral maps sit at ~68%, geo-referencing at ~49%, only 11 states/UTs have finished map digitisation, and ULPIN covers a bit over 30% of parcels. Meaning: the record that MATTERS in a boundary fight — the map — is precisely the one most likely to still need verification at the local revenue office. Cross-check every digital map against the official survey record before relying on it.

The clock you cannot ignore

Twelve years of open, hostile, continuous possession can EXTINGUISH your title through adverse possession — and a suit for possession based on title carries the same twelve-year limitation. An encroachment tolerated politely for a decade is quietly becoming the neighbour’s land. Acting early is not aggression; it is how the limitation law forces you to behave.

Example: A family “adjusts” with a neighbour’s shed two metres over the line for fifteen years, then sells — and the buyer’s survey triggers the fight. By then the neighbour’s adverse-possession clock has already run. The survey that cost a few thousand rupees in year one would have settled it while it was still a shed, not a claim.

What to do (and avoid) during a dispute

DO: pull RoR and cadastral map, confirm deed-vs-record survey number, file for the official survey at the Tehsildar’s office, negotiate with the report in hand, then notice, then court — asking for a commissioner when you get there. AVOID: self-help demolition or fence-moving (it converts your civil case into their criminal complaint), oral settlements with nothing recorded, and waiting out of politeness while the twelve-year clock runs.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Most reliable evidence? The cadastral map and FMB, plus a fresh official survey. Can maps hold up in court? Yes — weighed with title documents and other evidence. Survey doesn’t match my deed? Fix the record mismatch first; it undermines everything downstream. Can my neighbour claim my land by occupying it? After twelve years of open hostile possession — yes. Check records online? Text records usually; maps often still need the revenue office.

Bottom line

Boundary fights are won on certified measurements, not memories. Survey early, record everything, act inside the limitation clock — and treat the few-thousand-rupee survey as the cheapest insurance land ownership offers.

For you: For a plotting developer boundaries are the product itself — every plot you sell is a promise about a line. Fresh FMB-matched survey BEFORE layout design, stones planted and photographed at handover, and the survey report in each buyer’s file: that ritual is why organised ventures command premiums over the neighbour selling “by the tree line”. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 How to check if a plot is legally safe to buy

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: How to Check If a Plot Is Legally Safe to Buy in India — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • With plots, nobody upstream has checked anything — legal verification is entirely your job.
  • The 30-year title chain proves a seller CAN sell; the latest deed alone proves little.
  • An EC only catches REGISTERED transactions — deposit-of-deeds mortgages and court cases need separate searches.
  • ULPIN, the 14-digit parcel ID, catches an altered survey number.
  • The whole drill costs a few thousand rupees; a disputed title costs years.

Why verification matters MORE for plots

Buy a flat and a builder, a lender and usually a RERA registration have each done a slice of checking before you arrived. Buy a plot and NONE of that happened. Vacant land also carries a messier past — split among siblings after a death, handed around inside a family, converted from farmland years ago with paperwork that never caught up. Every one of those moments is a place a defect could have entered the record and stayed. Verification is finding them BEFORE you pay.

Start with the title chain, not the latest deed

A sale deed tells you who holds the land — nothing about whether the person BEFORE them had any right to hand it over, and that gap is where most disputes begin. Ask for the Mother Deed, then trace every sale, gift, partition and inheritance forward. Thirty years is the working standard — your bank will stop at a 13-year EC, but that is a LENDING threshold, not a title standard. Inheritance deserves special attention: in ancestral property every coparcener holds a share and ALL must sign; a guardian selling a minor’s share needs court permission; one absent signature can unwind a sale a decade later.

The EC catches less than you think

Owning land and being FREE to sell it are separate things. Pull the EC yourself from the sub-registrar for the full 30 years — in most southern states a clean result returns as Form 16, while Form 15 lists whatever is registered against the property. Now the part sellers rarely volunteer: the EC shows only REGISTERED transactions. A mortgage created by depositing title deeds with a bank often never reaches the register; neither will an unregistered sale agreement, an oral family partition, or unpaid dues. So run two more searches: the e-courts portal for litigation on the survey number, and the state land-records portal for the latest revenue record — comparing owner name, survey number, extent and classification against the deed. Any mismatch: investigate before proceeding.

Definition — Equitable mortgage (deposit of title deeds): a loan secured by simply handing the original deeds to the bank — legally valid, and frequently invisible on the EC. It is why “show me the ORIGINALS” is a due-diligence question, not rudeness: a seller without originals may have a banker holding them.

Zoning, conversion and RERA: what you can actually build

Start with the master plan — how is the parcel classified? Farmland once? Ask for the conversion ORDER and the fee receipt; a seller’s assurance is not a document. Then RERA: larger plotted developments generally must register, and an unregistered one becomes a problem YOU inherit. Then who may buy at all — agricultural-land eligibility varies by state. And layout approval sits with the planning authority — verified separately, on its portal, by its LP number.

Legal checks vs physical checks

CheckWhat it provesWhere you get it
30-year title chainThe seller’s right to sellSub-registrar copies + your advocate
EC (30 years)Registered loans/claimsSub-registrar / online
e-courts searchPending litigationecourts portal, by survey number/parties
Revenue recordState’s view of owner + classificationState land-records portal (Dharani in TG)
Licensed surveyGround matches paperSurveyor on site
ULPIN checkParcel identity not alteredState records where rolled out

Walk the boundary before you pay

A licensed surveyor measures the plot against the deed AND the revenue record — paper and ground drift apart more often than buyers expect. Where your state has rolled it out, check ULPIN — the 14-digit parcel ID under the national land-records programme, sometimes called Bhu-Aadhaar — which nails the parcel’s identity against an altered or misquoted survey number.

Warning signs of a risky purchase

Survey numbers that DISAGREE across deed, revenue record and EC. A PoA sale where the PoA is unregistered or does not explicitly authorise sale. A seller who produces photocopies every time and originals never. Land on a prohibited-property list — the Sub-Registrar will refuse it until the restriction resolves. And resistance to a NEWSPAPER NOTICE: publishing one inviting objections before closing is ordinary practice — a seller who argues against it is telling you something.

Example: The newspaper notice is the cheapest trap-detector in the kit: ₹3,000, one column, fifteen days. A silent fortnight closes the deal with confidence; a phone call from an unknown “co-owner” just saved you the whole purchase price.

Quick answers (FAQ)

How far back should the chain go? 30 years. Does the EC show every loan? No — registered ones only; deposit-of-deeds mortgages hide. Do all plots need RERA? Larger plotted developments generally yes; small ones may be exempt — check the threshold. Is patta proof of ownership? Supporting evidence only. Do I need a lawyer for a small plot? Yes — defect risk does not scale down with plot size; a small plot with a bad title is 100% bad.

Bottom line

Chain, EC, courts, record, zoning, survey — six checks, each catching what the others miss, none substituting for the rest. A few thousand rupees of verification against years of litigation: the cheapest insurance in real estate.

For you: This lesson is the customer’s-eye view of everything your school has covered — use it as your master checklist template. Better: run the six checks on your OWN ventures and publish the results as a buyer pack. In a market where verification is “entirely the buyer’s job”, the developer who does the job FOR the buyer owns the trust premium. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 2C patta explained: why assigned land drags buyers into CID cases

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: 2C Assigned Patta Explained: Why This Land Type Lands Buyers in CID Cases — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Assigned patta is government WELFARE land with strings attached — strings that survive every deed registered after the original allotment.
  • Restriction periods run 10–20 years by scheme — counted from the ALLOTMENT date, not from when the current seller acquired it. SC/ST-assigned land never loses its restriction.
  • Buyers get pulled into CB-CID cases as often as sellers: registering a deed on restricted land counts as participation, intent or not.
  • IPC 420, 465, 468 and 471 recur in these charge sheets — and 420 is NON-BAILABLE.
  • 2C patta (converted-for-residential land) and assigned patta are DIFFERENT things — agents blur them, and that blur alone has put buyers in front of investigators.
  • The patta’s remarks column and the A-Register hold everything you need — read both before any advance.

What assigned patta actually is

It reads like any other patta — survey number, name, extent, classification. The difference hides in a clause in the REMARKS section, or in the assignment order sitting behind the patta that sellers do not always show. Governments have allotted land to landless families, SC/ST beneficiaries, ex-servicemen and other eligible groups under welfare schemes — and the original assignment order carries the real terms: how long the beneficiary must hold, what the land may be used for, and whether government approval is needed before any sale.

What the conditions mean in practice

Restriction periods commonly run 10–20 years depending on the scheme — counted from the DATE OF ALLOTMENT, not from whenever the current seller took over. Violate them and the government cancels the patta and takes the land back — no warning, no compensation. The person who paid full market price is left with nothing, and on record the sale is treated as if it never legally happened.

Why purchases turn into CID cases

The script is always the same: a seller holds assigned land still inside its restriction period; the buyer is told the patta is clean or the condition “doesn’t apply anymore”; money moves, the deed registers. Then — sometimes years later — a neighbour complains or a revenue officer notices something during routine inspection. The Tahsildar orders an inquiry, confirms the violation, and the case moves to the police — often the CB-CID. The buyer’s registered deed is not a defence; it is EXHIBIT A.

The criminal sections that get invoked

IPC 420 — cheating by dishonest inducement, up to seven years, and NON-BAILABLE: arrest can precede a magistrate’s order. IPC 465 — forgery, where patta records were altered or a condition quietly omitted. IPC 468 — forgery for the purpose of cheating. IPC 471 — USING a forged document as genuine — the section that can reach a buyer who registered a deed without ever checking its underlying conditions. This is the lesson’s hardest truth: in assigned-land cases the buyer is treated as a participant, not merely a victim.

2C patta ≠ assigned patta

2C patta: agricultural land formally CONVERTED for residential use — its one condition (agricultural classification) already resolved through a conversion order. No welfare distribution behind it, no community restriction, no lock-in on sale. Assigned patta: welfare redistribution, with conditions on who may own, how long they must hold, and whether it can be sold at all. Agents in a hurry blur the two — and that blur alone has put buyers in front of investigators. If a seller says “2C”, verify it is a CONVERSION story, not an ASSIGNMENT story: the paper trail behind each is completely different.

Example: A buyer is shown a “2C patta — fully converted, free to sell”. The A-Register extract tells another story: allotted 2011 under a housing scheme to an SC beneficiary — an assignment, restriction unexpired and, being SC-assigned, never expiring. The “conversion” framing was the con. One ₹50 counter application at the revenue office exposed it.

How to verify before you pay

☐ Pull the LATEST patta/ownership record from the state portal.
☐ Get the A-REGISTER extract (or state equivalent) at the revenue office — it shows the original allotment date and scheme.
☐ Compute the restriction period from the ALLOTMENT date, not the seller’s purchase date.
☐ SC/ST-assigned? The restriction does not expire — check buyer eligibility before going one step further.
☐ Even past the window, some schemes require written Collector permission before registration — confirm.
☐ Review the ownership history: a pattern of quick, repeated transfers is exactly what CB-CID investigations flag.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Can assigned patta land be sold legally? Only where the scheme’s conditions allow — restriction expired, permissions obtained, or formal de-restriction granted. The non-alienation period? 10–20 years by scheme, from allotment; perpetual for SC/ST assignments. Why do BUYERS end up in cases? Because registering a deed on restricted land is treated as participation in the violation. If conditions are violated? Cancellation, resumption without compensation — plus the criminal exposure above.

Bottom line

This is the one land category where a mistake is not just money — it is a charge sheet. The remarks column and the A-Register cost minutes and rupees to read; read them before any advance, and walk away from any seller who resists the reading.

For you: Deepens your assigned-land gate with the criminal dimension: in Telangana assembly work, any parcel whose chain touches a government grant gets the allotment-order + A-Register/Dharani-remarks check BEFORE the agreement — because for a professional developer the reputational cost of one CID summons exceeds any parcel’s margin. And police the vocabulary in your own market: brokers saying “converted” when the record says “assigned” are handing you their liability. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

Sources

🎓 NRI guide to buying land in India: rules, restrictions, and what to know

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: NRI Guide to Buying Land in India: Rules, Restrictions, and What to Know Before You Invest — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Residential and commercial property: NRIs buy freely — no cap, no RBI approval.
  • Agricultural land, farmhouses and plantations: banned under FEMA, with three narrow exceptions.
  • Repatriating sale money depends on FEMA rules, where the purchase money came from, and tax compliance.
  • Long-term gains (held 24+ months) now taxed at 12.5% WITHOUT indexation — and NRIs miss the grandfathering relief residents got.
  • Buying from abroad is an information problem: due diligence must happen BEFORE the wire, not after.

Who counts as an NRI here

The definitions run through FEMA, 1999. An NRI is an Indian citizen living abroad — Indian passport overseas. An OCI is a foreign citizen of Indian origin — foreign passport, Indian roots. For property purchases the two follow essentially identical rules, so everything below applies to both.

What NRIs can and cannot buy

Freely: apartments, villas, houses, offices, shops — residential and commercial, any number, no RBI approval. Never: agricultural land, farmhouses, plantation property — a national ban, whatever the buyer’s income or country, and it covers every route: sale deed, agreement to sell, or any indirect structure trying to dress the purchase up.

Why the farmland ban exists

The restriction lives in FEMA’s capital-account framework — today the Non-debt Instruments Rules, 2019 plus the RBI Master Direction, not one neat section. The policy logic: farmland touches food security, and offshore money flooding in could distort prices and displace the people who actually work the land. RBI approval technically exists but is granted rarely, for genuine agricultural or public-interest cases — it is not an investment route. And breaking the rule is not a slap on the wrist: the transaction can be VOIDED, the Enforcement Directorate can step in, and penalties can reach three times the value.

The three exceptions

Inheritance — the common path: an NRI can inherit farmland from a resident Indian (or from another NRI with RBI clearance), and can later sell it — but only to a resident Indian. Gift from a specified relative — allowed if the giver is a RESIDENT Indian who qualifies as a specified relative under FEMA; NRI-to-NRI farmland gifting is barred. Prior ownership — farmland you owned while resident stays yours after your status changes; the ban bites only on NEW acquisitions. In all three cases, sending the eventual sale money abroad is not automatic — RBI guidance and tax compliance govern it.

The buying process, step by step

1) Confirm eligibility — your NRI/OCI status, and that the property is residential/commercial, not agricultural or plantation. 2) Shortlist RERA-registered projects and verify approvals with the authority. 3) Due diligence — 30-year title search plus EC, ruling out disputes, mortgages and legally non-transferable land. 4) Open the NRI account — NRE, NRO or FCNR; every rupee routes through it (cash is barred under FEMA and tax law alike). 5) Execute a PoA if you will not attend — embassy-attested or apostilled in Hague-convention countries, then stamped in India before use. 6) Sign the agreement on stamp paper with a payment schedule; register within four months. 7) Transfer funds and KEEP the Foreign Inward Remittance Certificate. 8) Pay stamp duty at the state’s rate. 9) Register the sale deed — in person or via the PoA holder — this is the ownership proof. 10) Afterwards: file tax on any rent, and keep records seven years.

Definition — NRE / NRO / FCNR: the three NRI bank accounts. NRE holds foreign earnings (fully repatriable), NRO holds Indian earnings (repatriation capped), FCNR holds foreign-currency deposits. WHICH account funded the purchase decides how freely the sale money can leave later.

Repatriation — getting money out later

From an NRO account, up to USD 1 million per financial year can go abroad, subject to tax compliance. Property bought with FOREIGN funds via NRE: full repatriation of sale proceeds, for up to two residential properties; beyond two, the money joins the USD-1-million pipeline. Bought with NRO (India-earned) money: capped and paperwork-heavy. The lesson runs backwards in time: the repatriation freedom you want at EXIT is decided by the account you use at ENTRY.

Example: Two NRIs each sell a Hyderabad flat for ₹2 crore. One bought via NRE with dollars — proceeds wire out cleanly. The other bought with NRO rupees — the same money now queues under the annual cap with Forms 15CA/15CB. Identical flats; the funding route made the difference.

Documents you will need

Identity: passport (with OCI card if applicable), PAN, proof of overseas residence. Property: title deed with 30-year history, EC, regulatory approvals, and for completed buildings the approved plan and occupancy certificate. Transaction: NRI-account statements, the FIRC, the attested PoA if buying in absentia, and Forms 15CA/15CB where remittances abroad cross ₹5 lakh a year.

Tax obligations

TDS turns on who SELLS: buy from a resident and TDS is the small 1% over ₹50 lakh; buy from another NRI and TDS applies at much higher rates regardless of value. Capital gains: property held over 24 months is long-term, taxed at 12.5% — and NRIs do NOT get the grandfathered indexation relief residents kept, so old purchases can carry surprisingly large taxable gains. Rent earned in India is taxed in India (standard deduction applies); tax treaties usually let you credit Indian tax against the home-country bill.

Buying from a distance — the real risk

The hardest part of absentee buying is the gap between what a listing claims and what the land is. Map overlays and verified survey numbers narrow that gap — classification, zoning, boundary, location — before you wire an advance or fly in. But no map reads a title chain, catches a forged deed, or gives FEMA and tax advice: a property lawyer and a tax advisor who handle NRI transactions remain non-negotiable, because a wrong classification does not just lose money — it can void the transaction.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Can an NRI buy restricted or assigned land? No — the assigned-land bans apply on top of FEMA. Any cap on how many properties? No cap on residential/commercial ownership; repatriation rules differ beyond two. Buy without visiting India? Yes — via a properly attested and stamped PoA. Sell inherited farmland? Yes, but only to a resident Indian. What if an NRI buys farmland in breach? Transaction voidable, ED proceedings, penalty up to 3× the value.

Bottom line

The rules are clear on paper and unforgiving in practice. Confirm the land’s classification BEFORE committing, route every payment through the right NRI account, and put a lawyer and tax advisor on the file — the two costs that are always cheaper than the mistake.

For you: NRI money is a major buyer pool for Hyderabad plots — and every NRI buyer’s first fear is exactly this lesson. A venture that hands NRI buyers a ready file (residential classification proof, RERA number, PoA template, TDS guidance) converts that fear into sales. Also: never let a broker pitch your FARM plots to NRI buyers — that deal voids itself. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 Joint Development Agreement: how builder and owner shares work

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Joint Development Agreement: How Builder and Owner Shares Work in India — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • A JDA trades the owner’s land for a share of what the builder makes of it — neither pays the other upfront.
  • Three sharing structures — area, revenue, profit — each with a different risk profile.
  • Owners typically end with 30–50% of the developed property, set by location and leverage.
  • Courts will not enforce an UNREGISTERED JDA. Registration is existential, not optional.
  • Section 45(5A) defers the owner’s capital gains to the completion-certificate year, not the signing year.
  • Most JDA disasters are builder-selection disasters — vet the builder harder than the draft.

What a JDA actually is

A structured trade: the owner contributes land — granting development RIGHTS, retaining ownership until later conveyances — and the developer builds at his own cost, returning a slice of the outcome as units or revenue. The owner unlocks idle land without selling; the developer gets prime land without buying. Four laws touch it at once — Contract Act, Transfer of Property Act, Registration Act, RERA — and the Registration Act point is decisive: skip registration and the agreement has no legal teeth at all.

The three sharing structures

Area sharing (commonest in residential): the built area — carpet, built-up or super built-up, whichever the agreement DEFINES upfront — divides by ratio; 40:60 on 100 flats leaves the owner 40 units to hold, rent or sell, keeping some shelter from a shaky market. Revenue sharing: the developer sells everything and remits the owner’s percentage — cash rather than keys, common in hot urban markets — where the drafting MUST nail gross vs net vs actually-collected, because loose words here breed most fights. Profit sharing: splits what remains after all costs; aligns incentives in theory, but a developer who inflates costs quietly shrinks the owner’s cut — workable only with real audit rights.

Definition — Carpet / built-up / super built-up: three ways to measure the same building — usable floor, plus walls, plus common areas. A ratio without a named measure is not a ratio; it is a future argument.

Typical ratios and what moves them

LocationTypical owner shareWhy
Prime Tier-1 corridors40–50%Scarce, expensive land = owner leverage
City periphery35–45%Land value still strong, competition higher
Tier-2 cities30–40%Land less scarce; builder carries more sales risk
Tier-3 / rural fringe25–35%Developer shoulders nearly all risk

Large parcels (5 acres+) swing ratios back toward the owner; higher FSI, residential use, and proximity to existing infrastructure all push the owner’s share up. The professional way to argue any ratio: reverse-engineer it from GDV — owner share ≈ land value ÷ (land value + all-in build cost + builder margin) — then fight over the inputs, not the emotion.

How the process runs

Negotiate model and ratio → lawyer drafts (sharing clause, timelines, a registered GPA scoped strictly to what the project needs) → REGISTER with the Sub-Registrar → approvals and RERA registration → construction → handover of units or revenue at completion. A mid-sized residential project: three to five years end to end.

Red flags in the draft

No registration — worthless in court. A bare percentage with no named units, floors or defined revenue base — the single biggest fight-starter. A loosely-drafted GPA handing the developer more authority than the project needs — hard to claw back. No penalty clause — delays go unremedied. No REVERSION clause — a defaulting builder means litigation instead of an automatic handback of development rights.

Definition — Reversion clause: the provision that returns development rights to the owner automatically on defined defaults — the difference between a bad year and a decade in court. Insist on it; a builder who resists it is telling you why.

Area vs revenue sharing — choosing

Area sharing suits owners planning to hold or rent, and shields them somewhat from market swings. Revenue sharing suits owners wanting liquidity who trust the builder’s sales machine. In either: define the measure, the base, and the audit rights in the first schedule — not in a WhatsApp message.

Tax implications

Section 45(5A): individual and HUF owners under a REGISTERED JDA pay capital gains in the completion-certificate year, not the signing year — fixing the old horror of owing tax before receiving a single flat. Section 194-IC: cash paid alongside the property share draws 10% TDS. The builder’s own share is stock-in-trade — business income when sold. GST: applies on units sold BEFORE the completion certificate; sales after it are immovable-property sales, GST-exempt — a timing lever both sides should understand.

Is the model reliable?

The structure holds; the BUILDER is the risk. A developer running out of money leaves a half-built project and a stuck owner. RERA registration, delivery record and financial health, checked before signing, prevent most of what goes wrong later.

Before signing — checklist

☐ Builder’s RERA status, delivery record, financial standing verified.
☐ Ratio names exact units / a defined revenue base — never a bare percentage.
☐ GPA scoped tightly to the project.
☐ Penalty clause on missed milestones.
☐ Reversion clause that functions automatically.
☐ Property lawyer AND tax advisor before signature — 45(5A), GST and TDS shape what actually lands in your pocket.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is JDA registration mandatory? For enforceability, yes — an unregistered JDA cannot be defended in court. Can the owner cancel on default? With a reversion clause, rights return automatically; without one, litigation. When does the owner pay capital gains? Completion-certificate year, under 45(5A), for registered JDAs.

Bottom line

A JDA unlocks land value without a sale — and succeeds or fails on two things: the builder’s track record, and drafting that contemplates failure calmly. Registration, a precise sharing clause, and a working reversion clause are the owner’s armour.

For you: Your home ground. Walk into every ratio negotiation with the GDV maths done — the side that computed the pie controls the split. Prefer area-sharing when you believe in the corridor more than the counterparty, revenue-sharing when the sales machine is the asset. And the two clauses that keep JDs out of courtrooms: registration immediately, reversion always. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

🎓 Raidurg’s ₹237 crore acre: madness, or underpriced?

Land School — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Everyone Called Raidurg's ₹237 Crore Land Price Madness. We Call It Underpriced. — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Two TGIIC e-auctions (28 May and 1 June 2026) raised ₹2,529 crore from just 11.38 acres in Raidurg — averaging ₹222 crore per acre.
  • The headline is the wrong number: at an effective FSI of 10, ₹237 Cr/acre works out to ≈ ₹5,500 per BUILDABLE square foot.
  • Add construction at ₹5,000–5,500/sft → landing cost ≈ ₹10,000–11,000/sft, against prime absorption at ₹18,000–25,000/sft. The margin is the headline.
  • The demand engine is structural: Hyderabad hosts ~20% of India’s GCCs and led the country in new GCC launches through 2025-26.
  • Raidurg appreciated ~80–95% in five years (~13–15% a year) — and the auction resets comparables for the whole micro-market, flowing to private deals over 12–24 months.

What actually happened

On 28 May 2026, TGIIC auctioned Plot 1A & 1/F — 6.29 prime multi-use acres in the Raidurg growth corridor. Gowra Ventures won at ₹237 crore per acre — ₹1,490.73 crore total — closing 70.5% above the ₹139 Cr/acre reserve, where comparable auctions usually run 30–50% premiums; the overshoot was credited to main-road frontage, a near-rectangular layout and the multi-use designation. Five days later Plot P4 — 5.09 acres in the SAME survey number — went to Vamsiram Builders at ₹204 crore per acre (₹1,038.36 crore), a 46.8% premium over the same reserve; slightly interior, U-shaped, fronting a 45-metre road instead of the arterial. Together: 11.38 acres, ₹2,529 crore, average ₹222 Cr/acre — 42% above last year’s ₹156 Cr/acre average across comparable Raidurg auctions. The commentary split into euphoria (“unstoppable”) and alarm (“peak market”) — and both missed the maths.

The number the headlines skipped: ₹5,500 per square foot

One calculation reframes everything. An acre ≈ 43,000 sq ft. Multi-use designation with effectively unlimited FSI — build at a conservative FSI of 10 and you get ≈ 4,30,000 sq ft of saleable area. ₹237 crore ÷ 4,30,000 = ≈ ₹5,500 per buildable square foot of land cost — beside Sattva Knowledge City, ITC Kohenur and Durgam Cheruvu, not an alarming number at all. Layer in construction: signature-grade high-rise runs ₹5,000–5,500/sft all-in. Landing cost ≈ ₹10,000–11,000/sft. And the finished product? Prime new residential in this micro-market absorbs at ₹18,000–25,000/sft. Build at eleven, sell at eighteen-to-twenty-five: the developer margin is not just intact — it IS the story. The land price is not the risk; it is already priced into a model that works.

Definition — FSI (Floor Space Index): how many square feet you may BUILD per square foot of land. The lens that converts a per-acre headline into the only number a developer can underwrite: cost per buildable square foot. Hyderabad’s effectively unlimited-FSI policy is precisely why its per-acre prices can look wild yet pencil out.

Why Raidurg specifically — and why it holds

Land stories are demand stories, so the real question is what generates ₹18,000–25,000/sft absorption here — and whether it is structural or cyclical. The answer has a name: Global Capability Centres. Hyderabad attracted 40 new GCCs in 2025 through October — the most of any Indian city for the period — and now hosts roughly 20% of India’s GCCs (355+ centres), taking 41–46% of fresh national setups through 2025-26 even as Bengaluru keeps the largest installed base (~900). India’s GCC count is projected past 2,400 by 2030 — 2.8 million professionals, ~USD 105 billion of output. Nationally, GCCs took 38% of office leasing in 2025 — 31.3 million sq ft, a record. Nearly all of Hyderabad’s share of that sits within a few kilometres of these auction plots — leases, salaries, relocating families — and that demand needs housing, retail and mixed-use nearby. Raidurg IS the nearby. Decisions made in New York, London and Singapore boardrooms, largely irreversible once a centre and its talent base exist: structural, not speculative.

The five-year appreciation story

Raidurg has appreciated roughly 80–95% over five years — about 13–15% compounded. No corridor compounds at 15% forever; the question is whether it reverts, plateaus or continues. If prime stock prices at ₹18,000–25,000/sft today and GCC-grade employment keeps growing within 3 km, a trajectory toward ₹35,000/sft in five years is not aggressive — it is what 13–15% from a ₹20,000 base arithmetically implies. Even moderated to 8–10%, the directional case stands.

The honest maths at HIGHER land prices

Stress-test it: suppose a future auction settles at ₹400 crore per acre. Same FSI of 10: land ≈ ₹9,300/sft; construction ≈ ₹5,500; landing ≈ ₹14,800–15,000/sft. At ₹18,000 realisation the margin compresses but the project works; at ₹22,000–25,000 — where the market is trending — it works comfortably. The reframe: the land is not expensive; it is being priced CORRECTLY for the first time — against the demand that exists now, not against a baseline set when GCC density was a fraction of today’s.

What it means for each player

If you are…The takeaway
A developer eyeing similar parcelsUnderwrite on ₹/buildable sq ft, never ₹/acre: model land cost, stress construction, validate exit pricing on registered evidence — if the spread holds, the deal is real
An investor in the corridorNot early innings, not an obvious ceiling: GCC employment in a supply-constrained pocket is a durable tailwind, not a cycle
A landowner nearbyTwo back-to-back government auctions at ₹204–237 Cr/acre RESET the comparables — flowing through to private deals over 12–24 months

Quick answers (FAQ)

Why did the price cross ₹237 Cr/acre? Frontage, shape, multi-use FSI and 70% competitive premium over reserve. Why did the two parcels differ? Configuration — arterial frontage and rectangle vs interior U-shape on a 45 m road. Is Raidurg at its peak? The FSI maths says priced-to-model, not bubble — the risk lives in execution and absorption pace, not the land line. What does ₹237 Cr/acre mean for a developer? ≈ ₹5,500/sft of buildable land cost at FSI 10.

Bottom line

Every big land transaction is an information problem: the data exists, the maths is simple, and the gap between headline and analysis is where people get misled into euphoria or fear. Two sophisticated bidders did the buildable-square-foot maths and called ₹5,500–9,300/sft an entry point, not a ceiling. Whether the next parcel prints ₹250 or ₹190 crore, the structural case — employment density, infrastructure, scarcity, the GCC pipeline — has not changed.

For you: The most directly USEFUL lesson in this school: the ₹/buildable-sq-ft discipline is exactly how you should underwrite plotting land too — your version is ₹/sellable-square-yard after roads and open space, never ₹/acre raw. And the last row of the table is YOUR seat: government auction prints anywhere near your belts reset landowner expectations 12–24 months out — track TGIIC auctions as an early-warning system for your own acquisition pricing. Original Deccan Ledger lesson; full source article on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Can I buy land in Telangana? Rules, ceilings, and the benami line

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Can I Buy Land in Telangana? — Rules & Restrictions — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Telangana has NO state-origin restriction and NO farmer-status requirement: any Indian citizen can buy agricultural or non-agricultural land in all 33 districts.
  • All categories accessible: agricultural, horticultural, residential, commercial, industrial.
  • Ceiling caps (Ceiling on Holdings Act 1973) still apply per family — wet 10–27 acres, dry 35–54 (up to ~65 in drought-prone zones).
  • Section 22-A blocks registration of government, ceiling-surplus, waqf and assigned land statewide — with 1+ crore flagged acres under committee review (GO Ms 98, Aug 2025), statuses CHANGE: re-verify just before registration.
  • Buying in another person’s name to dodge restrictions is BENAMI: 1–7 years rigorous imprisonment, fine to 25% of fair value, confiscation.

Who can buy

The clean answer: everyone Indian. Unlike states that restrict farmland to agriculturists, Telangana runs open — no domicile test, no farming background, every district from Hyderabad and Rangareddy to Warangal and the fringe belts. The Bhu Bharati Act 2025 modernised the records behind that openness, assigning each parcel an 11-digit Bhudhaar ID that makes outsider transactions cleaner.

The ceiling caps

Open does not mean unlimited. Under the Ceiling on Holdings Act 1973, a FAMILY (of five = one standard holding) may hold: irrigated (wet) land 10–27 acres depending on soil class A–D; dry land 35–54 acres by soil class E–K, stretching to ~65 acres in drought-prone zones. Holdings AGGREGATE across all districts toward one ceiling. Violations bring registration refusal — and excess land is seized as ceiling surplus.

Definition — Standard holding: the ceiling unit for a five-member family; larger families scale within limits. The cap counts everything the family holds statewide — scattering purchases across districts does not reset it.

Where 22-A cuts across

All 33 districts are open — but Section 22-A blocks registration of government-owned, ceiling-surplus, waqf and assigned land statewide. The moving part: GO Ms No. 98 (August 2025) created a committee to rationalise MORE THAN ONE CRORE flagged acres, so a parcel’s status can change on the portal — verify before negotiation AND re-verify just before registration.

The due-diligence checklist

☐ 22-A prohibited list + waqf status on Bhu Bharati BEFORE any negotiation.
☐ Title search — 12-year minimum via Bhu Bharati (agricultural) or IGRS (non-agricultural); 30 years for high-value purchases.
☐ Tahsildar clearance that the family’s AGGREGATE holding stays within ceiling limits.
☐ Converting agricultural to non-agricultural? DTCP/HMDA/revenue approval + NALA tax before registration.
☐ Stamp and register at the SRO — budget 4–5% duty, with biometric verification mandatory from January 2026.

The benami line — read it twice

Buying land in ANOTHER PERSON’S name to circumvent restrictions — origin, residency, occupation or tribal-area rules — is a benami arrangement under the Prohibition of Benami Property Transactions Act 1988 (as amended 2016). Penalty: 1–7 years rigorous imprisonment PLUS a fine up to 25% of fair market value; the property can be CONFISCATED and the deed cancelled. Power-of-attorney workarounds, ownership-mimicking 99-year leases and shell-company structures are not recognised — do not attempt them.

Example: The framing that keeps people honest: in Telangana there is almost never a reason to structure creatively, because the front door is open. Anyone proposing a name-lending or PoA structure for a purchase you could lawfully make directly is importing risk you do not need.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Do I need to be a farmer? No. Can NRIs buy? Non-agricultural yes; agricultural land is barred by FEMA regardless of the state’s openness (see the NRI class). How much can a family hold? Ceiling-dependent: 10–27 wet / 35–54 dry acres. The one check that cannot be skipped? 22-A — twice: at decision, and just before registration.

Bottom line

Telangana’s door is open; the discipline is inside the house — ceilings, the 22-A list, honest names on deeds, and a record system that now enforces all three.

For you: This is your one-lesson answer for every out-of-state or first-time investor who asks “can I even buy there?” — send them here. For your own assemblies, watch the CEILING maths on landowner families: a family already near its cap cannot take your developed-plot consideration as land without breaching it — structure JD returns accordingly. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Bhu Bharati: the portal that now runs Telangana’s land records — a map of every service

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Bhu Bharati Services Hub — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Bhu Bharati (the Record of Rights in Land Act 2025 system) replaced Dharani as Telangana’s agricultural land-records platform — restored 11-column Pahani, GPS-linked LPM identifiers, Bhudhaar parcel IDs, time-bound processing.
  • Its services split across two portals: land RECORDS on bhubharati.telangana.gov.in; REGISTRATION-side services (EC, market value, 22A list) on IGRS at registration.telangana.gov.in.
  • Fourteen services cover the whole land lifecycle: identity → records → checks → transaction → after-transaction.
  • Every service has its own full class in this school — this page is the map.

The system in one paragraph

Telangana digitised land records in waves — Maa Bhoomi, then Dharani (2020), then the Bhu Bharati Act (2025) after Dharani’s thin records and error legacy demanded a rebuild. The new system restored the detailed Pahani, tied every parcel to drone-surveyed LPM polygons and a unique Bhudhaar ID, moved corrections to time-bound Tahsildar decisions, and wired REGISTRATION itself to the record (no verified Bhudhaar, no slot). For anyone touching Telangana land, this portal — plus IGRS for the registration side — is where every question starts and ends.

The services, grouped as you’ll use them

StageServiceWhat it answers
Know the parcelLand Details (Pahani/1B) · Certified Copy · Search by NameWho owns it, what it is, on signed paper
Know its identityBhudhaar · LPM · Know Your Position · Bhu Naksha mapIs this ground THAT record?
Check before money22A Prohibited · EC (IGRS) · Market Value (IGRS)Can it sell, what rides on it, what the state values it at
TransactSlot BookingThe registration appointment, gated on the Bhudhaar
After / fixMutation · Auto-mutation check · RoR Correction · NALA · Application StatusGetting the record to say the truth, and tracking it

The order that keeps buyers safe

The hub’s own logic, and this school’s: 1) pull the record (Pahani/1B) and match the seller; 2) verify identity on the ground (LPM/Bhudhaar/KYP); 3) run the pre-money checks (22A first — is it saleable at all — then the 30-year EC, then market value for the duty maths); 4) only then the slot; 5) after registration, verify mutation and pull a fresh certified copy. Every step is free or nearly free, and every step has a dedicated class in this section of the school.

Example: The two-portal rule that resolves most confusion: RECORD questions (Pahani, mutation, Bhudhaar, corrections, NALA) → Bhu Bharati. REGISTRATION questions (EC, market value, prohibited list, deed copies) → IGRS. Searching the wrong portal is the single most common dead end on the new system.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is Dharani gone? Superseded for agricultural land — dharani.telangana.gov.in redirects; Dharani-era records migrated (with the gaps the correction window exists to fix). Do I need an account? Viewing is mostly open; transactions and certified copies use mobile-OTP registration. What about urban property? Registration-side services on IGRS; municipal records with GHMC/CDMA. Where do I start as a buyer? Land Details Search — then follow the order above.

Bottom line

One Act, two portals, fourteen services, one sequence. Learn the map once and every Telangana land question becomes a two-minute lookup instead of a favour someone owes you.

For you: This is the FIRST class to give anyone new on your team — the map that makes the other 13 guide classes make sense. Print the two tables, pin them at the office, and let “which portal, which service, which order” become muscle memory before anyone touches a live deal. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).
