📑 Pahani & RoR 1-B: Telangana’s two core revenue records

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Pahani RoR 1-B Telangana Land Buying Guide 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Pahani (Adangal) records the physical facts — owner, use, soil, survey number; RoR 1-B records the legal title, khata and the full ownership history.
  • Issued by the Tahsildar; free to view online; certified copies ₹35 (Pahani) / ₹25 (RoR 1-B) at MeeSeva, ready in ~15 minutes.
  • The Bhu Bharati Act 2025 replaced Dharani for agricultural land: 11-column Pahani, GPS-linked LPM numbers, mandatory annual updates.
  • RoR 1-B auto-updates the moment a sale deed registers — a seller whose “years-old purchase” still shows the old name never registered it. Walk away.
  • The online view is for research; only the certified copy works for banks, courts and registration.

What the two records are

Pahani — also called Adangal — is the Tahsildar’s record of a parcel’s PHYSICAL facts: who owns it, how it is used, the soil type, the survey number. RoR 1-B — the Record of Rights — is the village-wise OWNERSHIP register: legal title, khata number, and the full history of who held the land and how it changed hands. The simplest frame: Pahani tells you what the land IS; RoR 1-B tells you who it BELONGS to. You need both before you talk price with anyone.

Definition — Khata number: the revenue account number under which a holder’s parcels are grouped in the village register — the “account” the land sits in, distinct from the survey number which identifies the parcel itself.

How Telangana’s record system evolved

Digitisation began in the Maa Bhoomi era; Dharani took over in 2020 and put everything under one portal; and in 2025 the state passed the Bhu Bharati (Record of Rights in Land) Act, replacing Dharani for agricultural land with a tighter system — restored 11-column Pahani entries, GPS-linked parcel identifiers called LPM (Land Parcel Map) numbers, and mandatory annual updates. Dharani’s records were thin; Bhu Bharati mostly fixes that, though the transition has left data gaps worth knowing about.

How to check them (online, free)

Keep district, mandal, village and the survey/khata number ready — the search will not run without at least one. 1) Open dharani.telangana.gov.in or ccla.telangana.gov.in (both official; avoid third-party sites selling “reports” of a free record). 2) Click “Know Your Land Status” → “Pahani and ROR-1B” under Records of Rights. 3) Pick district, division, mandal, village; search by survey number, khata number, name or mutation date; captcha; Get Details. 4) Read, download, print — good for YOUR research, not for a court or bank file.

Getting certified copies (MeeSeva)

For any official purpose — loan, dispute, registration — the certified copy is the only version that counts. Go to the MeeSeva centre in the SAME mandal as the land (a different district’s MeeSeva has no jurisdiction); ask for the Adangal form (Pahani) or RoR 1-B application; pay ₹35 / ₹25; the operator issues a receipt with an application number (also sent to your mobile — keep it for tracking); collect in about 15 minutes. Pre-digitisation records are a separate application at the Tahsildar office and can take days.

Reading the RoR 1-B, field by field

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Khatadar’s nameLegal owner in the revenue registerIdentical to the seller’s name on the deed — even a spelling variant is worth clarifying
Survey / LPM numberParcel ID tied to the cadastral mapStand on the land and confirm boundaries; Bhu Bharati registrations now run on LPM numbers
ExtentArea in acres and guntasMatch the deed; independently measure when the numbers are close but not exact
Land classificationWet, Dry, Garden, Shikam, WasteDecides legal use; agricultural land needs NALA conversion before construction
Mode of acquisitionPurchase / inheritance / government assignment“Government Assignment” is a HARD STOP — that land cannot be sold
Liabilities / encumbrancesBank loans, mortgages, attachment ordersAny entry = a live claim on the title; do not buy until cleared

Good sign: one owner name throughout, no liabilities, classification matching the seller’s story, and an LPM that lines up with the parcel when you stand on it.

Common problems and the fix

Name on RoR doesn’t match the deed — mutation never happened; the SELLER completes mutation at the Tahsildar BEFORE you sign anything; never accept “we’ll fix it after”. Multiple names on one survey number — a paper partition never carried into the record; get the partition documents and a lawyer’s confirmation of each share; one missing co-owner signature kills the sale. Mode of acquisition says Government Assignment — stop; no instrument cures it.

Example: The auto-update rule is a built-in lie detector: RoR 1-B refreshes the moment a sale registers in Telangana. A seller claiming he bought the parcel in 2019 while the record still names his vendor is telling you — in the government’s own handwriting — that the purchase was never registered.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Are Pahani and RoR 1-B the same? Linked, not the same — cultivation facts vs ownership title; read both. Cost? Free online; ₹35/₹25 certified at MeeSeva. Validity? Current financial year; refreshes after every registered transaction. Which portal? dharani.telangana.gov.in / ccla.telangana.gov.in (agricultural records moving to Bhu Bharati).

Bottom line

Two free searches and ₹60 of certified copies tell you who owns the land, what it legally is, and what claims ride on it — before the first price conversation. Refuse any deal where the record and the seller’s story disagree.

For you: Make the Pahani + 1-B pull the FIRST act of every parcel conversation — before the site visit, before pricing. And flip it into sales: pull both records live in front of YOUR plot buyers; the developer who opens the portal unasked is the developer whose word needs no checking. 📑 Pattadar Passbook: Telangana’s Aadhaar-linked title document

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Pattadar Passbook Telangana Land Buying Guide 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • The Pattadar Passbook (PPB / e-Passbook / “Title Deed”) is Telangana’s primary ownership document for agricultural land, under the Rights in Land and Pattadar Passbooks Act, 2020.
  • Aadhaar-linked: it carries the owner’s photo, thumbprint and biometric identity — built to kill impersonation fraud.
  • Every bank in the state demands it before any agricultural loan.
  • It is SEPARATE from the sale deed — after purchase it must be transferred into your name via mutation; a deed without an updated Passbook is a legal grey zone.
  • The documented fraud: buyers who skip mutation have had previous owners RE-MUTATE the land under succession claims.
  • Agricultural registration in Telangana requires the buyer IN PERSON with biometrics — no GPA stand-ins.

What the Passbook is

The Revenue Department’s Aadhaar-linked record of who holds title to agricultural land — proof of legal ownership before any court, bank or department, recording the full ownership history against the parcel. Most buyers know they need a sale deed; fewer know the Passbook is a separate document that must be TRANSFERRED into their name afterwards through mutation. The deed registers the transaction; the Passbook is what the revenue system recognises. Hold a deed without the updated Passbook and government schemes, bank loans and any onward sale all hit a wall.

Definition — Pattadar: the recorded holder of the patta — the person the state’s register names as owner. The Passbook is the pattadar’s identity-linked certificate of that status.

The anti-fraud design

The Passbook carries photograph, thumbprint and Aadhaar-verified identity precisely so a stranger cannot pose as the owner. Two consequences for buyers: if the face in the seller’s Passbook is not the face across the table, investigate before signing anything; and under the RoR Act 2020, a Passbook fraudulently created for GOVERNMENT land can be cancelled by the District Collector, with criminal proceedings and resumption of the land — a Passbook is strong evidence, not an unconditional guarantee.

How to check one (free, online)

1) Open dharani.telangana.gov.in or bhubharati.telangana.gov.in — Bhu Bharati is the updated portal under the 2025 Act and has the more current agricultural records. 2) No login needed to view; open the search screen. 3) Select district, mandal, village; search by Passbook number (faster — pulls the full record) or survey number; have the first four digits of the linked Aadhaar ready. 4) Verify owner name, Aadhaar linkage, survey number, extent, land type, transaction history; download. The online view verifies; banks want the MRO-signed hard copy.

Getting it transferred after YOU buy

1) Attend registration in person — agricultural registration under the Telangana system needs the buyer’s physical presence and biometric verification; both sides appear; no General Power of Attorney stand-in. 2) Mutation is typically AUTOMATIC after registration; if it does not trigger, apply via the portal’s citizen services or at the Tahsildar with documents. 3) When mutation processes, check the new Passbook on the portal field by field — name, survey number, extent, Aadhaar. 4) The physical Passbook posts to your address after Tahsildar approval; track by application number. Lost one? Duplicate at the MRO with Aadhaar-linked mobile verification.

What the Passbook contains

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Pattadar name + photoAadhaar-linked identity of the ownerThe face must match the seller — a photo mismatch on a biometric document is a serious red flag
Aadhaar numberLinked identity numberCross-check against the seller’s physical ID
Survey + khata numberParcel and account identifiersVerify against boundaries and the deed — errors cascade into every later transaction
ExtentArea in acres/guntasMust match the deed; small discrepancies need a correction order BEFORE registration
Land typeWet / Dry / otherDrives loan eligibility and permitted use — read the column, not the seller’s description
Transaction & mutation historyEvery recorded ownership changeRecent, frequent activity deserves entry-by-entry scrutiny

The documented fraud pattern

The most recorded scam under Dharani: a buyer registers the sale but delays mutation — the revenue record still names the previous owner, who then applies for mutation AGAIN under succession or other grounds, effectively reclaiming the Passbook. Thousands of genuine buyers have faced this. The rule is absolute: mutation immediately after registration — even a week’s delay is a window.

Example: Sequence for every purchase: register (in person, biometric) → confirm auto-mutation triggered the same week → verify the new e-Passbook online → receive the physical book → THEN treat the purchase as complete. The deal is not done at the SRO; it is done when the Passbook says your name.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is the Passbook the same as the sale deed? No — deed records the transaction, Passbook records recognised ownership; you need both, in your name. Cost? Free to view online; duplicates via the MRO. Expiry? None — it updates after every registered transaction. Why do banks insist on it? It is the state’s biometric-verified title record — the cleanest collateral check that exists here.

Bottom line

In Telangana the Passbook IS ownership as the state sees it. Verify the seller’s before you pay; chase your own the week you register; and treat any gap between deed and Passbook as a live risk, because the fraudsters certainly do.

For you: For your JD assemblies: every landowner partner’s Passbook should be pulled and face-matched at the first meeting — it kills the two worst surprises (imposters, and un-mutated inheritors who cannot yet sign). And when your buyers’ ventures involve farm plots, walking them through their own Passbook transfer converts a legal chore into a loyalty moment. 📑 Adangal: the cultivation register that exposes what title records hide

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Adangal Telangana Land Buying Guide 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Adangal (Pahani / Village Account) records cultivation: soil, crops, irrigation, tenancy, classification — Part II of the Record of Rights, updated annually by the MRO.
  • RoR 1-B is legal title; Adangal is ACTUAL USE — and a tenant in years-long occupation is a dispute no sale deed erases.
  • The wet / dry / waste classification is the field buyers ignore and sellers count on them ignoring — price follows the column, not the pitch.
  • Land Nature must read PATTA to be privately transferable — Sarkari = government land; Assigned = welfare land that cannot be sold to you.
  • Free on Dharani/CCLA; ₹35 certified copy at MeeSeva.

What the Adangal is

The Tahsildar’s cultivation register for every agricultural parcel in a village: who cultivates, what crops, which soil, what irrigation source, whether a tenant is involved, and the land’s classification. It functions as Part II of the Record of Rights — the cultivation side of the same system the RoR 1-B’s title side belongs to. People treat the two as interchangeable; they are not. For a buyer, the Adangal often reveals what the title record keeps quiet.

Definition — Fasli: the revenue year used in cultivation records. The Adangal’s crop entries are recorded fasli by fasli — a parcel’s recent faslis show who has actually been farming it.

The classification money hides in

Wet land is irrigated — commands higher prices, faces stricter use restrictions. Dry is rain-fed. Waste has no cultivation history — and in Telangana sometimes turns out to have no legal private ownership at all. Before agreeing any price, open the Adangal and read the Land Nature and classification columns yourself: the seller’s valuation often assumes a classification the document does not support.

How to get it

Online (free): dharani.telangana.gov.in or ccla.telangana.gov.in → “Land Details Search” / “Know Your Land Status → Pahani and ROR-1B” → district, division, mandal, village → search by survey, khata or linked Aadhaar → captcha → read the Land Nature column FIRST, then the cultivator name against the seller’s. Certified (₹35): MeeSeva in the land’s own mandal; application number to your mobile; ~15 minutes for current records — and insist on the MRO-signed version; an unsigned printout has no official weight. Pre-digitisation Adangals: apply at the Tahsildar, allow days.

Reading it, field by field

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Survey / hissa numberParcel IDCross-check site and cadastral map — mismatch = boundary risk
Khatadar nameRegistered pattadarMust match the seller exactly — mismatch means mutation never completed
Land classificationWet, Dry, Garden, Shikam, WasteSets legal use and honest value — read it yourself
Land NaturePatta / Sarkari / AssignedPatta transfers privately; Sarkari and Assigned do NOT — one word can end the deal
Crops & cultivationLast fasli’s crops, irrigation, tenancyReveals active tenants with possession claims; also drives crop-loan / PMFBY eligibility
Soil classificationSoil type and water rateFeeds productivity value and the bank’s assessment

Good sign: Land Nature = Patta, khatadar = seller, no tenant in the cultivator column, classification consistent with the pitch, survey number pulling up clean.

The traps, with fixes

Assigned sold as private — welfare land distributed to landless farmers cannot be sold, full stop; corridor sellers around Hyderabad routinely pitch assigned parcels as investments; the Adangal exposes it — the moment you see Assigned, stop; any deed on it is void from execution. Active tenant in the cultivator column — someone else has been farming, possibly for years; long cultivation creates de facto possession claims in Telangana courts; get a legal opinion on the tenancy BEFORE agreeing anything. Waste priced as wet — verify classification on the portal before any price talk. Old khatadar name — mutation never filed; seller fixes it at the Tahsildar first. Forged offline Adangal — documented cases include fake cultivation records supporting fraudulent sales (one disputed patta parcel was sold as a 43-plot venture with no planning approval); pull the survey number on the portal YOURSELF and compare every field.

Example: The one-word audit: open the Adangal, read Land Nature. “Patta” — continue the conversation. “Sarkari” or “Assigned” — the conversation is over, at any discount, with any paperwork.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Adangal vs Pahani? Same document, two names. Adangal vs RoR 1-B? Use vs title — read both. Cost? Free online; ₹35 certified. Why does a plotting buyer care about crops? Because tenancy, classification and Land Nature decide whether the parcel can ever legally become plots.

Bottom line

The Adangal is the cheapest lie detector in Telangana land: one free search shows the tenant the seller forgot to mention, the classification the price ignores, and the one word — Assigned — that no lawyer can fix.

For you: Add a fixed “Adangal line” to your parcel checklist: Land Nature / cultivator vs seller / classification vs pitch. Ten seconds per parcel, and it catches the two deal-killers (assigned taint, sitting tenant) earlier than any title search. 📑 Title deed & Mother Deed (Mula Deed): tracing Telangana’s chain of title

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Title Deed Mother Deed Telangana: Complete Guide 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • The Mother Deed (Mula Deed / Parent Document / Root of Title) is the FIRST registered document that established ownership — every later deed links back to it.
  • Only a registered deed transfers title (Registration Act 1908 + TP Act 1882); an unregistered instrument is inadmissible under Section 49.
  • Certified copies: ₹200 each; online 1–2 days via IGRS; manual SRO copies 10–15 working days.
  • IGRS holds digitised deeds from 1 Jan 1983 ONLY — older transfers need a manual index-book search at the SRO. A blank portal result is NOT a clean title.
  • Supreme Court (SLP (C) 1866/2024, decided 7 May 2025): a registered sale deed alone does not guarantee ownership if the seller’s underlying title is defective.

What the Mother Deed is

Telangana’s property market runs on what the Registration Department calls LINK documents. The Mother Deed — locally the Mula Deed — is the start of that chain: the moment a property first entered registered ownership, through a government grant, revenue patta, court decree, partition or sale. Every gift, sale or settlement afterwards connects back to it. Buyers confuse the Mula Deed with the LATEST sale deed; they are different things — one root per property, many branches accumulating over decades. A 30-year title search begins at the root and traces every transfer forward; a missing or broken link anywhere is a serious defect.

Definition — Link document: any registered deed forming one link in the ownership chain between the Mother Deed and today’s seller. Banks asking for “link documents” want the whole chain, not just the newest deed.

Checking the chain online (IGRS)

1) Create a Citizen account on registration.telangana.gov.in — Aadhaar-linked mobile, name spelled EXACTLY as on Aadhaar (variations cause download mismatches). 2) Open Deed Details / Certified Copy services → district → SRO → document number + year → Get Details; a second no-login cross-check runs at ccla.telangana.gov.in/deedDetailsWs.do. 3) Verify EVERY field against the physical deed: document number character by character; nature of deed (a sale deed and an agreement of sale are NOT the same); executant and claimant names vs Aadhaar; survey number, extent, boundaries, village, mandal — one mismatch needs explaining before you proceed. 4) Pay ₹200 per certified copy; the digitally signed PDF arrives in 1–2 days with full legal weight. Note: agricultural-land records are moving to the Bhu Bharati portal — confirm which portal covers your property type first.

The pre-1983 trap and the offline route

IGRS digitisation starts at 1 January 1983. Older transfers exist only in physical index books — so a blank portal result on an old property means “search manually”, never “clean”. Offline: 1) find the correct SRO — jurisdiction follows the PROPERTY, though since a 2024 update Hyderabad city buyers may use any of the 11 city SROs (Banjara Hills, Charminar, Secunderabad…); 2) submit a written application with document number, approximate year, parties, survey number — for pre-1983, specifically request the manual index-book search; 3) fees: ₹200 per certified copy; EC to 30 years ₹200, beyond ₹500; carry Aadhaar and any existing deed; 4) manual certified copies take 10–15 working days and carry the sub-registrar’s seal — fully admissible in banks and courts.

What a registered deed must show

FieldWhat it refers toWhy it matters
Document number + yearSRO identifier at registrationMust match the paper deed exactly — one character off is a fraud signal
Nature of deedSale / Gift / Partition / Settlement / Court decreeA registered SALE DEED transfers title; an agreement of sale does not
Executant & claimantSeller and buyer legal namesCross-check Aadhaar; deeds from 2001 on carry photos + thumbprints (s.32A)
Property scheduleSurvey no., extent, all four boundariesMust match the ground — hire a surveyor if measurements differ at all
Mode of acquisitionHow the seller got titleTraces the link into the chain above it

Why the chain outranks the newest deed

The Supreme Court’s 2025 ruling says it plainly: a registered sale deed does NOT guarantee ownership when the seller’s own title is defective. Registration certifies the transaction happened — not that the transferor had anything to transfer. That is why the lawyer’s 30-year search exists, and why it starts at the Mula Deed instead of the latest photocopy.

Example: A Shankarpally parcel: clean 2016 sale deed, verified on IGRS. The chain search finds the 1998 link was a partition deed one brother executed for land the family never actually partitioned — every deed after it inherits the defect. The 2016 paper was genuine; the title behind it was not. The root decides.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is the Mother Deed one document? Yes — one per property; everything else is links. How far should the search go? 30 years standard, always reaching the root. What does a certified copy cost? ₹200 (online 1–2 days; manual 10–15 days). Portal shows nothing? If pre-1983 — manual search; never assume clean.

Bottom line

Buy the chain, not the deed. Root to today, link by link, every field matched — with the manual index books doing the years the portal cannot see.

For you: Your acquisition rule: no agreement until the chain file (Mula Deed → every link → today) sits complete in YOUR folder, not promised from the seller’s. And archive your ventures’ complete chains digitally — the developer who hands a buyer’s bank the full link set in one PDF closes loans weeks faster than the market. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Bhu Bharati land details: reading the restored 11-column Pahani online

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Bhu Bharati Land Details: Check Pahani and ROR 1B Online 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • The restored 11-column Pahani shows pattadar, extent, classification, mode of acquisition AND the enjoyment column; the 1B is the ownership extract drawn from it.
  • The ENJOYMENT column records who actually occupies and cultivates — a mismatch with the seller is the classic sign of a tenancy or possession dispute.
  • Search runs on three identifiers: old survey number, new LPM (drone resurvey), Bhudhaar — if one fails, try the next before assuming the record is missing.
  • Viewing is free; banks and registrars accept ONLY the digitally signed PDF with QR and Bhudhaar.
  • Registration itself now runs on this record — the slot will not open without a verified Bhudhaar.

What the record shows

The Pahani (village account) and RoR 1B are Telangana’s core land records, and Bhu Bharati’s restored 11-column format returns the detail Dharani stripped out. Most people read the owner name and stop; the land’s legal health lives in the columns beside it. Two matter most for buyers: enjoyment — who actually occupies and cultivates (mismatch = presume a possession dispute until proven otherwise); and mode of acquisition — government-assigned land carries transfer restrictions no sale deed can cure.

Definition — Enjoyment column: the Pahani’s record of actual occupation — who is physically on and farming the parcel, as distinct from who holds title. The gap between the two names is where tenancy claims live.

How to check, step by step

Online: 1) bhubharati.telangana.gov.in — register with mobile + OTP, using the SAME name spelling as your documents so later transactions don’t bounce. 2) Land Details Search → Pahani or ROR 1B → district, mandal, village (wrong village is the top reason searches return nothing). 3) Enter survey number, LPM or Bhudhaar + captcha; if the survey number fails, switch to the LPM printed on the Pattadar Passbook — resurveyed parcels often answer to it first. 4) View free; pay the small fee for the digitally signed PDF with QR and Bhudhaar — screenshots and plain printouts are rejected by banks and registrars. Offline: MRO with the survey number or Passbook; request the SIGNED copy, not a photocopy of a screen; MeeSeva prints are fine for a first look only.

Field-by-field reading

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Pattadar nameRecorded ownerMatches seller and deed exactly, spelling included
ExtentArea of the holdingTallies with deed and physical measurement
ClassificationWet, dry, shikam + soil dataSets permitted use, value, conversion needs
EnjoymentWho occupies / cultivatesMismatch = possession dispute signal
Mode of acquisitionInheritance / purchase / assignmentAssigned = transfer restrictions; trace it
Bhudhaar / LPMParcel identifiersBoth must point at the exact parcel you are buying

Good sign: the record loads instantly, downloads as a signed PDF with QR and Bhudhaar, and shows an enjoyment column matching the seller.

The four stuck-search failures, and fixes

“No records found” — usually migration lag (often mid-NALA sync), rarely deletion: search by the Passbook’s LPM, retry in days, then the MRO. Record not digitally signed — invalid for sale or loan: ask the MRO to apply the Tahsildar’s signature. Survey number stops working — re-mapped to an LPM in the drone resurvey: find the LPM via the passbook and search with that. Name carried over wrong — a Dharani-era spelling error that quietly blocks loans and registration: file RoR correction at the Tahsildar before the 13 April 2026 deadline.

Why this record decides the deal

Telangana has tied REGISTRATION itself to this record: the slot will not open without a verified Bhudhaar, and every lender pulls the Pahani before sanctioning a plot or farm loan. Read it twice — once online before the site visit, once ON SITE against what you see; the column that disagrees with reality is the one that costs lakhs later.

Example: The live-pull test: ask the seller to open the record on the portal in front of you. A seller who refuses, or explains away an enjoyment mismatch verbally, has answered your due-diligence question — walk away.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Pahani vs 1B? Full 11-column record vs the ownership extract — read both. Free? Viewing yes; the signed PDF costs a small fee. Survey number shows nothing? Probably re-mapped — use the LPM. Which copy do banks accept? Only the signed PDF with QR and Bhudhaar.

Bottom line

The record IS the deal now — registration and lending both run on it. A clean, signed, ground-matching Pahani is not paperwork; it is the asset.

For you: Standardise the two-read ritual across your team: portal read before the site visit, printed record IN HAND during it, enjoyment column checked against whoever is standing on the land. The question “then who is that farming it?” has saved more Telangana buyers than any lawyer’s opinion. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Certified Pahani copy: the digitally signed PDF that banks actually accept

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Certified Pahani Copy Telangana: Download the Signed PDF 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • The certified copy = the Pahani/1B as a digitally signed PDF under the Tahsildar’s signature, with QR code and Bhudhaar embedded.
  • It is the ONLY version accepted for loans, registration and legal proceedings — screenshots and plain printouts are rejected outright.
  • Two preconditions: the underlying record must be signed, and its details must match your deed exactly.
  • The fee is small and per-copy; lenders want a FRESH download near any transaction.
  • Anyone selling to “arrange” a certified copy off-portal for a premium is selling a free download — or a forgery.

Why only the signed copy counts

The logic is anti-fraud: a screenshot can be edited in minutes, while a signed PDF is cryptographically tied to the record and its QR lets anyone verify it against the portal in seconds — so institutions stopped accepting anything less. Two things must be true for your download to work: the record itself carries the Tahsildar’s digital signature, and the details match your deed to the character. An unsigned record produces an unsigned copy — the single most common rejection at loan scrutiny.

Downloading it

Online: 1) pull the Pahani/1B on bhubharati.telangana.gov.in (survey number, LPM or Bhudhaar) and confirm every field BEFORE paying; 2) choose the CERTIFIED option, not the plain view; 3) pay the statutory fee, download the signed PDF, save the payment reference with the file; 4) scan the QR yourself and confirm it resolves to the same record — before handing it to anyone. Offline: MRO/MeeSeva counter — insist on the signed version (a stamped photocopy of a screen is not a certified copy) and scan the QR before leaving the counter.

What banks check on the copy

CheckWhy it mattersFail means
Digital signature validTies the copy to the TahsildarRejected outright
QR resolves to portalCopy is live and uneditedTreated as forged
Bhudhaar presentLinks copy to the exact parcelIdentity of the land in doubt
Name matches deedSpelling-level comparisonLoan stalls on discrepancy
Recent download dateInstitutions want current stateAsked to re-pull

Common failures, and fixes

Certified-copy problems are really RECORD problems. Comes back unsigned — the signature was never applied: request it at the MRO, then re-download. QR fails or mismatches — stale/tampered copy or sync lag: re-download fresh; persisting mismatch goes to the MRO. Name differs from deed — Dharani-era spelling error surfacing at the worst time: file RoR correction before the 2026 deadline, then re-download. Bhudhaar shows temporary — parcel-ID verification incomplete: pursue permanent status first; institutions increasingly check it.

When to pull a fresh copy

Three moments, always: before LISTING land for sale; before any LOAN application; and within two weeks AFTER registration — to confirm mutation carried your name in correctly. Keep the copies dated and filed: a trail of signed PDFs across time is quiet, powerful evidence if a dispute ever asks what the record said, and when.

Example: ₹45 of downloads over three years — at purchase, at loan, at listing — is a complete, timestamped, government-signed history of your parcel. The cheapest litigation insurance in Telangana.

Quick answers (FAQ)

What makes a copy “certified”? Tahsildar’s digital signature + QR + Bhudhaar in one PDF. Do banks take plain printouts? Never. How do I verify a copy someone hands me? Scan its QR; if it doesn’t resolve to the same record, it is worthless. How recent must it be? Fresh near any transaction — current state, not last year’s snapshot.

Bottom line

One habit covers everything: never transact on an unsigned record, and never trust a copy whose QR you have not scanned yourself.

For you: Build the dated-PDF archive for every parcel you hold — purchase, loan, listing, plus an annual pull. And when selling plots, hand each buyer a fresh signed copy in their closing kit: it pre-answers the bank’s first question and marks you as the venture whose records are always transaction-ready. 📑 Searching Telangana land records by name

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Search Telangana Land Records by Name on Bhu Bharati — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Name search finds records by the pattadar’s recorded name within ONE village — a shortlist tool, never proof.
  • Three uses: inherited land where the family knows the village but not the survey number; lost-document holdings; and checking how much a SELLER really owns in a village.
  • Rural Telangana repeats names heavily, and spellings differ across Aadhaar, passbook and record — search every variant.
  • It runs village by village: the right name in the WRONG village returns nothing, which buyers misread as “no holdings”.
  • Confirm every hit with father’s name → extent → khata → survey/LPM → Bhudhaar before relying on anything.

When to search by name

Three situations make it invaluable: inherited land — the family knows the village, nobody knows the survey number; old holdings whose papers are lost; and pre-purchase checks on how much land a seller actually holds where they claim to. The catch is precision: names repeat, transliterations drift, and a search under one spelling can miss the record filed under another. Treat every result as a shortlist to be confirmed, never a conclusion.

How to run it

Online (free): 1) bhubharati.telangana.gov.in → Land Details Search → record type → district, mandal, VILLAGE (it only runs inside one village at a time); 2) switch to search-by-name; enter the name as on the passbook or old records — retry Telugu-style and English transliterations, initials expanded and contracted; 3) scan the rows, shortlisting by father’s name and expected extent (common names return dozens); 4) open the shortlisted record and NOTE its survey number, LPM and Bhudhaar — all future checks run on those, never on the name. Offline: the MRO’s pattadar index allows more spelling flexibility than the portal — carry the passbook, Aadhaar or an old Pahani, and leave with identifiers written down, not just a nod.

Narrowing common names

DiscriminatorWhere it appearsWhat it settles
Father’s nameRecord and passbookSeparates namesakes within a village
ExtentRecordMatches the claimed holding size
Khata numberRecordGroups all parcels under one holder
Survey no / LPMRecord and mapPins the exact parcel on the ground
BhudhaarRecordFinal, unique confirmation

Good sign: a shortlisted record whose father’s name, extent and khata all agree with the documents in front of you — confirmed by LPM.

The four predictable failures, and fixes

Too many identical names — filter by father’s name and extent, confirm by LPM. Spelling variant misses — retry transliterations. Holder is deceased — records keep the late pattadar’s name until mutation completes: for inherited land, expect the grandfather’s or father’s name, then check mutation status. Zero results — usually the wrong village (boundaries also moved in the resurvey): confirm the current revenue village at the MRO and search again.

Why buyers run it even with the survey number in hand

A name search on the SELLER shows every parcel they hold in the village — which closes the neighbouring-parcel trick, where a clean parcel is shown and a disputed neighbouring one is registered. For inherited land it is step one of a chain: locate the record → check whether mutation moved it to the heirs → only then discuss any sale.

Example: Red-flag test: a seller who resists a simple name search of their own holdings — or whose claimed land appears under NO spelling in the stated village — is not ready for your advance.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Can I find land with just a name? Yes — free, within a chosen village. Search shows nothing — is the land unrecorded? Not necessarily: try variants, confirm the village, check whether it still stands in a deceased relative’s name. Is a name match enough to buy on? Never — confirm survey number, LPM and Bhudhaar. Can I see everything one person owns? Within one village, effectively yes; statewide views are not public — repeat per village.

Bottom line

Name search opens the door; identifiers close the deal. Use it to FIND records and audit sellers — then switch immediately to survey/LPM/Bhudhaar for everything that matters.

For you: Two professional uses: audit every landowner partner’s FULL village holdings before a JD (their other parcels’ disputes have a way of visiting yours), and use it in land-buying negotiations — knowing the family holds 12 acres nearby, not just the 2 being discussed, changes what you offer and how. 📑 Bhu Naksha: Telangana’s cadastral map, rebuilt by drones

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Bhu Naksha Telangana 2026: Cadastral Map Check & Download Guide — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Bhu Naksha (cadastral map / survey sketch / Tippon offline) shows every parcel’s shape, boundary and area on one GIS view — under the Survey and Boundaries Act 1923, now the Bhu Bharati Act 2025.
  • The 2025 statewide DRONE survey rebuilt the map from scratch — every parcel flown, photographed, tied to GPS — creating the LPM number alongside the old survey number.
  • In boundary fights the Tahsildar now uses the GPS-anchored LPM map, not the old paper sketch.
  • Free instant view online; certified Tippon from the MRO takes 7–15 days and is what the registrar accepts at sale-deed registration.
  • Sub-divided or merged in the resurvey? The old survey number alone will NOT pull the map — find the new LPM via your Passbook first.

What Bhu Naksha is

The visual half of the land record: the Passbook says who OWNS the land; the Bhu Naksha shows exactly WHERE it sits, what shape it has, which survey numbers touch its boundary, and how far it stretches on each side. Without the map, ownership paper never tells a parcel’s full story.

Definition — Tippon / FMB copy: the certified measurement sketch of a single survey number issued by the Survey department — the offline, legal-weight counterpart of the online map view.

Checking it online (free)

1) bhubharati.telangana.gov.in → GIS / “Cadastral Map” under Information Services — no login to view (the mobile app stays more stable than the desktop site during peak office hours). 2) District → Mandal → Village; the map zooms to the village with all survey numbers shaded. 3) “Search Survey Number” on the GIS toolbar — the parcel highlights; click it for the recorded extent in acres-guntas. 4) Print/download the PDF — and cross-check the same parcel on the ISRO Bhuvan Telangana viewer for an independent satellite overlay.

Getting the certified Tippon (offline)

MRO or MeeSeva → FMB Copy / Tippon application (issued by the Survey department under the 1923 Act) → form with survey number, village, mandal + Passbook or deed copy → the Mandal Surveyor processes after Tahsildar approval. Fees vary by service (one district lists ₹295 for sub-division applications, ₹1,410 for demarcation appeals). 7–15 days; the copy carries the surveyor’s seal and signature — the version the registrar accepts at registration. Tip: apply for the village map and Tippon TOGETHER; combined requests cost less.

Reading the map, field by field

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Survey number / sub-divisionLegal parcel identifierMatch exactly to the seller’s passbook entry
LPM numberGPS-anchored resurvey IDConfirm PERMANENT, not Temporary
ExtentArea in acres-guntasCompare with passbook — post-drone, ground reality wins
Boundary linesShape + adjoining survey numbersWalk all four sides; check encroachment
Land classificationWet, Dry, Shikam, governmentReject Shikam / government shading without clearance
Village / mandal / districtAdministrative locationAll three must match the deed

Good sign: closed boundary, Permanent LPM, extent matching the passbook, no overlap with neighbours or government colour shading.

The six map problems, and fixes

Survey number returns blank — sub-divided/merged in the drone survey or record in a holding state: search by Passbook number for the new LPM, then ask the Tahsildar for record reconciliation. Online extent ≠ passbook — the GIS survey applies a “ground reality rule” (drones measured 1.95 acres where paper said 2.00 → record becomes 1.95): file RoR Correction before the 13 April 2026 deadline if the gap is unfair. Parcel overlaps government/assigned land — the map can reveal slivers over Shikam, Waqf, forest or 22-A-flagged land: pull the village’s Prohibited Lands list; if the survey number appears, walk away. Seller shows an old printed Tippon — fraud rings have used outdated sketches to sell government land as private: refuse any map older than the April 2025 cutover; demand a fresh live download in your presence. Map vs ground mismatch — fences and walls drift over years: use Know Your Position — stand on each corner with GPS on. Two parcels show overlap — a record conflict; resolve at the MRO before any deal.

Example: The “fresh map or no meeting” rule: any boundary conversation runs on a map downloaded TODAY, on someone’s phone, in everyone’s presence. Old printouts are how government land gets sold as private.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Bhu Naksha vs Tippon? Online view vs certified offline sketch of the same map. Cost? Viewing free; Tippon fees vary — confirm at the MRO. Why did my survey number stop working? Resurvey re-mapping — use the LPM. Validity? Indefinite; auto-updates each survey cycle.

Bottom line

Paper says who owns; the map says WHAT exists. After the drone resurvey, the GPS-anchored map is the boundary truth Telangana’s own officers use — check it before every deal, and never against an old printout.

For you: For a plotting developer the Bhu Naksha is the FIRST feasibility tool: shape, road-touch, neighbours and government shading — visible before a single site visit. Overlay it with your Land Scout map layers, and make the fresh-download-in-presence rule non-negotiable on both sides of your deals. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 The Bhudhaar number: one parcel, one ID

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: The Bhudhaar Number in Telangana, Explained — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • The Bhudhaar is Telangana’s unique parcel ID, generated against GPS-mapped boundaries — two parcels cannot share one, which is what makes double registration hard.
  • It exists in TEMPORARY and PERMANENT states — and the registration slot opens only against a verified permanent Bhudhaar.
  • It travels with the PARCEL, not the owner: sales change the pattadar; the Bhudhaar persists.
  • It must print identically on the record, the passbook, the certified copy and the (new) sale deed.
  • Sources describe its digit count differently — treat the number on YOUR record as authoritative and quote it exactly.

What it is, and is not

Its job is disambiguation: survey numbers repeat across villages, subdivisions blur, paper descriptions drift — the Bhudhaar cuts through by binding ONE number to ONE mapped polygon of ground. Parcels enter the system with a temporary ID; verification of boundaries and records upgrades it to permanent — and everything downstream (the slot, increasingly lender scrutiny) keys on the permanent state. It is the parcel’s Aadhaar in spirit: identity for land, persistent across owners.

Definition — Temporary vs permanent Bhudhaar: temporary = the parcel is in the system but its boundary/record verification is incomplete; permanent = verified. Registration refuses temporary or absent IDs — which is why the status check comes before any money.

Finding yours (two minutes)

1) Land Details Search on bhubharati.telangana.gov.in with survey number or LPM — the Bhudhaar prints on the record itself. 2) Read the STATUS: permanent or temporary. 3) Cross-check the Pattadar Passbook carries the SAME number as the live record — a mismatch needs resolving before any deal. 4) Download the signed certified PDF — it embeds the Bhudhaar for anyone verifying later; lenders and buyers will ask for exactly this.

Where it appears — all must agree

LocationRoleIf it disagrees
Pahani / RoR 1BThe live record’s identifierThe record is the reference point
Pattadar PassbookOwner’s documentUpdate the passbook via the MRO
Certified copy PDFEmbedded with the QRRe-download; verify the QR resolves
Sale deed (new)Binds deed to parcelCorrect BEFORE execution, not after
Slot bookingPrecondition checkSlot refuses temporary or absent IDs

Good sign: a permanent Bhudhaar printing identically on record, passbook and certified copy, quoted exactly in the draft deed.

The four problems, and fixes

Stuck at temporary — verification incomplete: pursue it via the MRO; the slot will refuse until permanent. Record shows no Bhudhaar — a migration gap (often with missing LPM linkage): raise at the Tahsildar with passbook and old records. Passbook and record differ — one predates a correction: align the passbook to the live record at the MRO. Deed quotes it wrong — a transcription slip that unravels the parcel linkage: fix the DRAFT; post-registration cures are painful.

Why buyers check it first

The cheapest fraud filter in Telangana: a parcel whose ID is absent, temporary or mismatched CANNOT complete registration — so every rupee advanced before the check is a rupee at risk. Make it step zero: confirm permanent status, confirm the number matches across record and passbook, screenshot the status for your file (so it shows when you checked), and only then spend money on deeper diligence.

Example: The seller hurrying you toward an advance while the Bhudhaar sits temporary — “it will sort itself out” — is asking you to fund THEIR verification problem. Verified first, money second.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Why does registration need it? The agricultural slot opens only against a verified permanent Bhudhaar. Where is mine? On the Pahani/1B and the Passbook; the certified PDF embeds it. Does it change on sale? No — it belongs to the parcel; only the pattadar name changes. Record and passbook differ? Resolve at the MRO before any transaction.

Bottom line

One number, one polygon, one status check — the thread that joins paper to ground. Permanent and matching everywhere, or the deal waits.

For you: Add a “Bhudhaar line” to your parcel sheet: number + status + screenshot date. In assemblies, run it across ALL survey numbers on day one — a single temporary-status parcel in the middle of your layout is a registration bottleneck you want to discover before the design, not before the launch. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 The LPM number: mapping old survey numbers to the drone resurvey

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: LPM Number in Telangana: Map Survey Numbers to the New System — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • The LPM (Land Parcel Map) number is the identifier from Telangana’s GIS drone resurvey — each record linked to an exact mapped boundary.
  • Bhu Bharati is shifting from survey-number to LPM-keyed records, with the Bhudhaar as the unique ID riding on top.
  • Old deeds, ECs and family memory speak survey numbers; the live record increasingly answers to the LPM FIRST.
  • Subdivided family land: one old survey number fans out into SEVERAL LPMs — one per parcel, each with its own Bhudhaar. Normal.
  • The #1 “my record disappeared” complaint is just a survey-number search on a parcel that now answers to its LPM.

Why the LPM exists

A century of subdivisions, merged holdings and hand-drawn village maps left survey numbers pointing at ambiguous ground. The resurvey replaced that ambiguity with polygons measured from the air and verified on foot. The transition is the tricky part: both identifiers remain meaningful — what matters is confirming they refer to the SAME parcel.

Finding yours and mapping it back

Have the passbook? Start there — the LPM prints on the Pattadar Passbook for resurveyed parcels and is the fastest search key on the new portal. Then: search the record by LPM, confirm extent and pattadar match expectations; the record displays the LEGACY survey linkage — note both together, because your deed and EC still speak the old number; and quote the pair (plus Bhudhaar) in every diligence note and draft. Searches failing? Try each identifier in turn (survey → LPM → Bhudhaar; migration gaps affect them unevenly); ask the MRO to look up the survey-to-LPM mapping from resurvey data; and a parcel with NO LPM linkage gets flagged at the Tahsildar before any transaction.

Survey number vs LPM

AspectSurvey number (legacy)LPM (resurvey)
SourceHistoric village surveyGIS drone resurvey
PrecisionDescriptive, drift-proneMapped polygon boundaries
Where citedOld deeds, ECs, memoryLive record, passbook, portal
SubdivisionsFractions of one numberSeparate LPM per parcel
Future roleLegacy referencePrimary search key

Good sign: a record where LPM, legacy survey number and Bhudhaar display together and match the deed’s description.

The four transition frictions, and fixes

Survey number finds nothing — the parcel answers to its LPM now: search with the passbook’s LPM; the record is almost certainly there. No LPM anywhere — the resurvey linkage hasn’t landed: raise at the Tahsildar; do NOT transact on an unlinked parcel. One survey, many LPMs — subdivided land fanned out: identify YOURS by extent and boundary before proceeding. Deed and LPM extents differ — small drifts are normal resurvey reality; large gaps need survey clarification first.

What a diligence file now carries

The triple, verified as one parcel: legacy survey number from the deed + LPM from the record + Bhudhaar as the unique key. A file with only the old number is running on the previous system’s assumptions. On the ground, the LPM is what the GPS position tool and the cadastral view key on — it lets you stand on the land and confirm you are inside the right polygon.

Example: Red flag: a seller whose paperwork speaks only old survey numbers and who cannot produce the LPM or Bhudhaar is selling something the new system cannot yet see. Resolve that before money moves.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is the survey number obsolete? Not yet — deeds and ECs cite it; the record shows the linkage; the LPM is becoming primary. Why several LPMs for one survey number? Subdivision → separate parcels, each with its own ID. My search fails — is the record gone? Almost never: use the passbook’s LPM. LPM vs Bhudhaar? Map identifier vs unique transaction ID — together with the legacy number they form the parcel’s full identity.

Bottom line

Speak both languages: the deed’s survey number for history, the LPM for the live system, the Bhudhaar to bind them. One parcel, three names, one polygon.

For you: Update your land-bank register NOW: add LPM and Bhudhaar columns beside every survey number you track, and resolve the blanks at the MRO this month — not during a deal. In the Scout sheets, an LPM-complete parcel is a faster-closing parcel; price your urgency accordingly. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Know Your Position: the GPS check that kills the two-fields-over trick

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Know Your Position: The Telangana GPS Land Check — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Know Your Position overlays your phone’s GPS on the cadastral map — showing which survey number / LPM polygon you are standing inside, with surrounding boundaries.
  • It collapses one specific deception: the mismatch between the land SHOWN and the land DOCUMENTED.
  • Orientation, not adjudication: consumer GPS drifts metres (more under trees) — legal boundaries stay with survey records and licensed surveyors.
  • Run the check at the parcel’s CENTRE, not its edges — mid-parcel readings defeat GPS drift.
  • Free, five minutes, done before any money conversation.

What it does

A site-visit verification tool in the Bhu Bharati ecosystem: record, seller and your own feet cross-checked in one glance. When that becomes routine, the two-fields-over trick — showing a beautiful parcel while the deed describes the scrubby one behind it — simply stops working. Use it inside its limits: a reading near a boundary line is a question, not an answer.

Using it on a site visit

1) Open the tool via the portal ON SITE with location allowed — LOAD IT BEFORE ARRIVING; rural signal varies. 2) Walk to the parcel’s middle — centre readings defeat drift; edges invite it. 3) Read the survey number / LPM under your position; SCREENSHOT with the location visible. 4) Match against record, passbook and draft deed identifiers — any mismatch stops the conversation until explained.

What GPS can and cannot settle

QuestionCan GPS settle it?What settles it properly
Am I on the documented parcel?Yes, at parcel centreReading + record match
Is this boundary line exact?NoSurvey records; licensed surveyor
Does shown land match the deed?Yes, as a first checkFull identifier match in diligence
Where does the neighbour’s land start?Indicatively onlyFMB sketch; demarcation survey
Is the extent as claimed?Roughly at bestMeasurement survey

Good sign: a centre-of-parcel reading matching the record’s survey number and LPM exactly, screenshotted, filed beside the documents.

Field realities, and fixes

Drift near boundaries — read from the centre; treat edge readings as inconclusive. No signal on site — load beforehand, or capture coordinates and verify after. Reading disagrees with the seller — the polygon on screen is not the documented parcel: STOP; this is precisely the fraud the tool exists to catch. Map feels misaligned — rare base-map anomalies: cross-check the FMB; raise persistent cases at the MRO.

Why every site visit needs it

It costs nothing, takes minutes, and closes the one gap no document can: whether the ground under discussion is the ground on paper. Keep the output with the file — a dated screenshot of the reading beside the record extract is a tidy, persuasive piece of a diligence trail.

Example: Red flag: the seller who keeps the walk at the parcel’s edge, discourages the GPS check, or explains a mismatched reading with “map errors” is showing you land they do not intend to sell you.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is it legally conclusive? No — indicative; boundaries come from survey records and demarcation surveys. Accuracy? A few metres, worse under cover — centre readings reliable, edges not. Without signal? Load in advance or verify captured coordinates later. Extent check? Only roughly — measurement surveys settle extent.

Bottom line

Five minutes at the parcel’s centre, one screenshot, one match against the record — and nobody can show you one parcel and sell you another.

For you: Standing operating procedure for your team’s site visits: KYP screenshot from parcel centre + fresh Bhu Naksha download, both timestamped into the parcel folder before any negotiation. And use it defensively on YOUR ventures — walk buyers to their plot’s centre and let their own phone confirm the plot; trust built by the buyer’s own device outlasts any brochure. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Auto-mutation: when the system updates the Pahani for you — and when it doesn’t

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Dharani Auto Mutation Telangana Guide 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Auto-mutation: agricultural sale-deed registration at the SRO automatically triggers the Pahani/RoR update to the buyer’s name — no separate application, under the 2020 RoR Act, now Bhu Bharati Act 2025.
  • Before Dharani, deed and revenue record lived in two offices with no link; integration fixed that — in principle.
  • In practice sync delays, migration errors and mismatches have left THOUSANDS of Pahanis showing the old owner after registration.
  • Seven days is the unofficial ceiling — beyond it, the deed-vs-Pahani gap is a documented risk, and the grievance portal is your lever.
  • A Pahani still naming the old owner is an open door for that person to apply for mutation under succession claims.

What auto-mutation is

Under the Telangana Rights in Land and Pattadar Passbooks Act 2020 (carried into the Bhu Bharati Act 2025), completing agricultural sale-deed registration at the Sub-Registrar triggers the portal to update the Pahani to the new buyer — no separate Tahsildar application. In 2025 Bhu Bharati replaced Dharani as the primary agricultural portal (dharani.telangana.gov.in now redirects); the auto-mutation concept carries forward, but migrated records may have gaps — so if a recent registration is not updating, check bhubharati.telangana.gov.in before concluding it is merely delayed.

Checking your mutation (under two minutes)

1) Land Details Search → district, mandal, village, survey number → the Pahani should show YOUR name as Khatadar; the previous owner’s name means auto-mutation has not processed. 2) “Track Application” with the registration document number or application ID — pending / in-process / completed; “in process” after seven working days means the trigger has stalled (old Dharani-era IDs remain searchable on Bhu Bharati). 3) Stuck? Citizen Services → Grievance Redressal / Correction of Land Records — file with document number, registration date, survey number, and the registered deed attached. 4) After processing, confirm the Pahani shows your name, Passbook number and survey details — and screenshot/download the updated record immediately as DATED proof. Then apply for the updated e-Passbook: a Passbook in the old owner’s name breaks every future transaction, including loans.

The offline escalation (Tahsildar/MRO)

If the grievance stalls: 1) go in person to the mandal’s Tahsildar/MRO with the ORIGINAL deed and a copy of the portal’s “in process” screen. 2) Request manual correction — under Bhu Bharati this is handled at Tahsildar level (no Collector approval), cutting the wait. 3) Submit deed, Aadhaar, Passbook number, survey details; take the acknowledgment receipt. 4) Get the ORDER NUMBER and track it under RoR Corrections / Grievance Status; physical follow-up every 10 days is reasonable. Suspect a competing claim by the previous owner? Say so explicitly and ask for the survey number to be put under observation — do not rely on the portal in contested cases.

Confirming it completed — field by field

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Khatadar nameCurrent recorded ownerYOUR name exactly as on the deed
Passbook numberNew owner’s linked PPBNew e-Passbook generated; old PPB deactivated
Survey number + extentThe parcel and its areaExtent matches the deed — errors cascade into loans
Mode of acquisitionHow ownership arrived“Purchase” + the registered transaction date
Mutation dateWhen the change recordedMust POST-date registration; earlier = data error
Transaction historyChain of ownership changesEnds with the previous owner selling to YOU; gaps = incomplete chain

Good sign: your name as Khatadar, mutation date within 7 days of registration, mode = Purchase, extent matching the deed, new e-Passbook generated with Aadhaar linked.

Why the 7-day window matters

Every day the Pahani still names the seller is a day the seller (or their heirs) can file a competing mutation under succession or other grounds — the documented Dharani-era fraud. The system closed the two-office gap; the sync delays reopened a smaller one. Your job is to close it fast: check on day 3, escalate on day 8, and never file the purchase away as “done” until the record and Passbook both say your name.

Example: Calendar discipline: registration Monday → portal check Thursday → if unchanged the following Monday, grievance filed with the deed attached → ten days later, Tahsildar in person. Buyers who treat mutation as an event, not a consequence, are the ones the fraud never finds.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Do I need to apply for mutation after buying farmland? Normally no — it auto-triggers; your job is VERIFYING it happened. How long should it take? Within ~7 days. Stuck longer? Grievance online, then Tahsildar manual correction. Does this cover non-agricultural property? No — urban mutation runs separately through the municipality.

Bottom line

Auto-mutation is a promise, not a guarantee. Verify at day 3, escalate at day 8, screenshot everything — and the system’s convenience becomes real instead of theoretical.

For you: Build the 3-day check into your closing workflow for every acquisition — assistant task, not memory. And for your plot buyers: a one-page “after registration” card (check Pahani day 3, grievance path, e-Passbook application) turns the state’s weakest step into your venture’s best after-sales moment. Original Deccan Ledger guide lesson; full source guide on 1acre.in (links above).

📑 Mutation on Bhu Bharati: the auto path for sales, the Tahsildar path for inheritance

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Mutation in Telangana on Bhu Bharati: Sale and Inheritance — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Mutation = updating the Record of Rights after ownership changes. Under the RoR Act 2025: sales auto-mutate; inheritance, succession and court-decree cases are filed with and decided by the Tahsildar.
  • The sale sequence is tight: registration against a verified Bhudhaar → transaction flows to the land-records module → Pahani reflects the buyer within DAYS.
  • Auto-mutation only works when registration ran on the verified Bhudhaar — mismatched-identifier deals are exactly the ones whose Pahani never updates.
  • The old climb to the Collector is gone — Tahsildar decides, which is why timelines shortened.
  • Until the Pahani and passbook carry the new name, the previous holder can still borrow against or resell the land on paper.

The two paths

Purchase (auto): the sub-registrar slot only opens on a verified permanent Bhudhaar, which links the deed to the parcel — that linkage IS what makes the automation work. Register, note your document number (your tracking key), wait days not months, then pull the Pahani and check your name, extent and classification character by character; the new pattadar passbook follows the updated record — keep the old owner’s documents until yours arrive. Inheritance (manual): heirs file at the village’s Tahsildar with the death certificate, legal-heir certificate and passbook; the Tahsildar may verify with family members and local enquiry before passing orders; track by application number, then pull the updated Pahani and passbooks for EACH heir.

Documents required

DocumentUsed forNotes
Sale deedSale mutationAuto path; keep the document number
Death certificateInheritanceMandatory for succession cases
Legal-heir certificateInheritanceFamily Member Certificate from MeeSeva
Pattadar PassbookBothOld passbook surrendered; new one issued
AadhaarBothIdentity and eKYC of the parties
Court decreeDecree casesCertified copy with proof of finality

Good sign: a Pahani showing the new holder within days of registration, extent unchanged, and a passbook issued in the SAME spelling as the deed.

When mutation stalls — four causes, four fixes

Pahani not updated after sale — registration ran on a mismatched or temporary Bhudhaar: raise via application status, then the Tahsildar with your document number. Record still names the deceased — no succession filing was ever made: file the inheritance case with heir proof; the record will not fix itself. Extent shrinks after mutation — a data-entry slip in the carry-over: file RoR correction IMMEDIATELY; do not drift toward the 2026 deadline. Family dispute freezes the case — contested succession goes to enquiry: expect a hearing; a registered family settlement speeds resolution.

Why it matters after registration

Plenty of buyers stop at registration and discover years later that the Pahani still names the seller — who has since mortgaged the land. The deed protects you in COURT; the record protects you in the MARKET; you want both. Diarise it: registration day → Pahani check within two weeks → escalation if nothing moved. Ten minutes closes the loop on the entire purchase.

Example: Red flag: any seller or agent calling mutation “a formality you can sort out later”. The window where record and deed disagree is exactly where the frauds live.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Is mutation automatic now? For registered sales against a verified Bhudhaar — yes, within days. How do heirs mutate? Tahsildar filing with death certificate + heir certificate + passbook. How long? Sales: days; inheritance: weeks; anything past a month deserves escalation. Does mutation prove ownership? No — ownership flows from the registered deed; mutation aligns the record with it. Cost? Modest statutory charges — treat large “facilitation fees” as a red flag.

Bottom line

Sale mutation is automation you VERIFY; inheritance mutation is a filing you MAKE. Neither is optional, because the un-mutated window belongs to the previous owner’s creditors and schemes.

For you: In JD assemblies the inheritance path is your critical path: land in a late father’s name cannot even sign your agreement until heirs mutate. Ask “whose name is the Pahani in TODAY?” at the first meeting, and where it is a deceased elder, budget the Tahsildar timeline into your deal calendar — or watch your launch date slip a season. 📑 Legal Heir Certificate (Family Member Certificate): who inherits, on paper

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: Legal Heir Certificate Telangana 2026: MRO & Meeseva Apply Guide — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • Processed in Telangana as the Family Member Certificate (FMC): the Revenue Department’s official record of a deceased person’s surviving spouse, children, parents and dependents.
  • It serves NON-litigious claims — pensions, insurance, PF, land mutation; a court-issued Succession Certificate (Indian Succession Act 1925) is the separate document for recovering financial assets.
  • Hindu Class-I heirs (spouse, sons, daughters, mother) inherit equally; Class-II (siblings, grandparents) only if no Class-I exists.
  • Vineeta Sharma v Rakesh Sharma (2020): daughters hold coparcenary rights BY BIRTH, married or not — omitting them makes later sale deeds voidable.
  • Jurisdiction follows the deceased’s LAST RESIDENCE, not where the property sits; wrong office = automatic rejection.
  • The Telangana HC (Global Industries) is clear: the MRO certificate identifies family members — it cannot decide contested titles; disputes go to civil court.

What the certificate is — and its court-issued cousin

Two documents overlap and get confused. The MRO/Tahsildar Legal Heir Certificate (technically the FMC, under the GHMC Act 1955 and land-revenue rules) suits routine claims and the initial MUTATION of land under the Passbook Act. A Succession Certificate comes only from the Civil/District Court and is what banks and institutions demand for recovering financial assets. Both can apply to the same estate at the same time, depending on what is being claimed. The FMC names every heir under the personal law applicable to the deceased.

Definition — Class-I heirs (Hindu Succession Act 1956): the first circle — spouse, sons, daughters and mother — who inherit EQUALLY and exclude everyone else. Siblings and grandparents (Class-II) enter only when no Class-I heir exists.

Applying online (MeeSeva)

1) Register at meeseva.telangana.gov.in — the same account handles caste, residence and FMC services. 2) Services → Revenue Department → Family Membership Certificate — the form lists deceased details, applicant details, and a ROW FOR EVERY HEIR (name, relationship, age, gender). 3) Upload: death certificate, applicant’s Aadhaar, relationship proof (birth/marriage certificate), latest address proof, ration card if available, deceased’s Aadhaar — affidavits from ALL heirs declaring no objection speed verification. 4) Pay the MeeSeva charges; note the reference number. The Tahsildar, VRO and Mandal Revenue Inspector verify and conduct FIELD inquiries within 15–30 days — the download unlocks the moment the Tahsildar approves, so check status daily after day 15.

Applying offline (MRO)

1) Go to the MRO of the deceased’s LAST RESIDENCE — jurisdiction is fixed by residence, not property location. 2) Fill the form on ₹2 stamp paper: deceased’s full name, date of death, last address, complete heir list; attach death certificate, all heirs’ IDs, address proof, ration card; photos of all major heirs are usually asked. 3) Take the stamped acknowledgment with reference number — the VRO will verify the heir list with NEIGHBOURS at the residence. 4) After the enquiry clears, the Tahsildar signs and issues — demand the seal-and-signature page in clear original colour; banks routinely reject faded photocopies.

What it contains, field by field

FieldWhat it meansWhat to check
Deceased’s name + date of deathWhose estate this isMatches the death certificate — no spelling variants
Last permanent addressAnchors jurisdictionMust fall under the signing Tahsildar’s mandal
List of legal heirsEvery spouse, child, dependent parentMarried daughters LISTED — their omission is the top defect
Relationship + age + genderClass determination; pension/guardian useNo minor child omitted, even under a different guardian
Issuing authorityTahsildar / MROSeal of the mandal where the deceased last lived
Date + reference numberSystem identityVerify the certificate is LIVE on MeeSeva by its number

Good sign: every Class-I heir including married daughters, the right mandal’s seal, and a reference number that verifies live on the portal.

The classic defects, and fixes

Married daughters left off — a century of habit, now legally fatal: sale deeds built on a defective FMC can be set aside on suit; before signing anything, demand inclusion of ALL daughters. Tahsildar rejects a Class-II application — officials cannot mechanically refuse siblings/grandparents; where no Class-I heirs exist they must inquire — file a written representation citing the High Court’s position and escalate. Contested estates / NRI heirs — the FMC route is for the undisputed; contested inheritance goes to a declaratory suit in civil court.

Example: Buyer’s use of this lesson: when purchasing inherited land, read the FMC yourself and COUNT the daughters. Three sons signing while two married sisters exist somewhere is not a discount opportunity — it is a future partition suit with your plot in the middle.

Quick answers (FAQ)

FMC vs Succession Certificate? Revenue-office family list vs court authority over financial assets — different jobs, sometimes both needed. Timeline? 15–30 days with field verification. Can the MRO decide who owns the property? No — identification only; title disputes are civil-court business. Do all heirs need to consent to a sale? Yes — every heir the certificate names (and every one it wrongly omits) must sign.

Bottom line

The FMC is the gateway document of inherited land: get it complete (daughters included), from the right mandal, verified live — and treat any incomplete heir list as a countdown to litigation.

For you: Standing rule for buying from families: FMC + your own daughter-count + every heir’s signature (or registered release deeds) before any agreement. The missing married daughter is the single most common time-bomb in Telangana land deals — and the cheapest one to defuse at paperwork stage. 📑 RoR correction: one year to fix the record — closing 13 April 2026

Telangana land document guide — an original Deccan Ledger lesson · source & further reading: ROR Correction in Telangana: File Before 13 April 2026 — 1acre.in

Key Highlights

  • RoR correction fixes Dharani-era errors — misspelled names, wrong extents, missing survey/LPM linkages, misclassification — filed at the Tahsildar.
  • The window is ONE YEAR from the Act’s commencement (14 April 2025), closing 13 APRIL 2026 — after which entries attain finality.
  • Four correctable buckets: identity, quantity, location, character. Ownership DISPUTES are not corrections — those go to civil remedies; deceased-on-record is MUTATION, not correction.
  • Authority sits with the Tahsildar (appeals to RDO and Collector); decisions are time-bound.
  • Old official paper wins: pre-Dharani pahanis, deeds, passbooks, survey records.

What the window covers

The Act’s bargain is simple: one clean year to challenge and repair the migrated record, after which casual correction closes. Generous compared to the Dharani years — brutal if you sleep on it. Correctable errors fall in four buckets: identity (name spellings, father’s name), quantity (extent mismatches), location (missing or wrong survey/LPM linkages), character (wet shown as dry, agricultural shown otherwise). Every week of delay stacks your case behind thousands racing the same date — and a rejection near the deadline may leave no time to cure and refile.

Filing at the Tahsildar

Paper wins these cases — assemble proof FIRST. 1) Pull the current Pahani and 1B and mark exactly which entries are wrong; precision here shapes the whole case. 2) Assemble proof: old pahanis, the sale deed, passbook, Aadhaar, survey records — the older and more official, the stronger. 3) File with proof at the Tahsildar; keep the NUMBERED acknowledgment — your tracking key. 4) Attend if called, answer the enquiry, then pull the corrected record and check EVERY character — confirm the fix did not introduce a new error.

Errors and their winning proof

ErrorWinning proofWatch out
Name misspelledAadhaar, passbook, old PahaniThe fix must match the DEED spelling too
Extent wrongSale deed, survey sketch, old recordsNeighbouring claims can surface
Survey number missingOld Pahani, FMB, resurvey LPM dataOften an LPM-linkage issue, not loss
Wrong classificationOld Pahani, revenue recordsAffects value and conversion rights
Deceased still on record—That is MUTATION: file succession instead

Good sign: an acknowledged filing well before the deadline, proof that PREDATES Dharani, and a corrected record verified character by character.

Why corrections fail (process, not merit)

Wrong case type — succession and ownership contests filed as “corrections” bounce: inheritance → mutation; disputes → civil remedies. Thin proof — an application that merely asserts the right spelling goes nowhere: attach old pahanis and deeds. Missed hearing — non-appearance lets the case lapse quietly: track the number, attend every date. The fix breaks something else — verify the fresh record immediately and refile within the window if needed.

Why the deadline outranks everything

After 13 April 2026 the record you have is PRESUMED the record that is right. Loans, sales and registrations will run against it; unwinding an error shifts from a Tahsildar form to an uphill appeal with a heavier burden. For sellers an uncorrected record kills deals at scrutiny; for buyers it kills the loan. If you hold Telangana land and have not pulled the record since migration — do it this week. The check is free; discovering the error after finality is not.

Example: Red flag: anyone advising you to leave a known record error “for after the deal” or after the deadline is trading YOUR legal position for THEIR convenience. File first, transact second.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Deadline? 13 April 2026. Who decides? Tahsildar; appeals to RDO/Collector. What can be corrected? Names, extents, survey/LPM linkages, classification. After the deadline? Finality clause — appeals only, slower and costlier. Sell with a pending correction? Technically possible, practically painful — mismatches stall registration and loans.

Bottom line

One free record-pull this week versus an appeal ladder next year: audit every parcel you own or plan to buy against the deadline, and let the calendar — not the counter clerk — drive the urgency.

For you: URGENT for the land bank: pull the Pahani/1B for EVERY survey number you or your landowner partners hold, THIS MONTH, and file corrections on anything off by a letter or a gunta. Post-deadline errors become pricing discounts on your own inventory. Also screen acquisition targets: a parcel needing correction after finality is a longer, cheaper deal — negotiate accordingly.
