🏆 Pahani & RoR 1-B — the complete masterclass

Masterclass — a Deccan Ledger deep-research edition · sources credited at the end

Key Highlights

  • Telangana runs on TWO parallel record systems for every parcel: the Pahani (the village’s annual working diary of the land) and the RoR 1-B (the ownership register). Reading only one is reading half the truth.
  • Both descend from a 150-year lineage — Salar Jung’s reforms → the Land Revenue Act 1317 Fasli (1907) → sethwar, tippan and village map → the 1954-55 khasra pahani → the 1971 RoR Act → 1989 passbooks → Dharani (2020) → the Bhu Bharati Act (2025). Old generations of the record still WIN cases today.
  • The law’s big promise: every entry in the Record of Rights is PRESUMED TRUE until the contrary is proved — a presumption, not a guarantee.
  • The law’s big limit, from the Supreme Court down: revenue entries prove POSSESSION and support claims — they do not by themselves confer, create or extinguish TITLE. Title comes from registered deeds, grants and statutory vesting.
  • Dharani (2020) thinned the record and dropped the enjoyment column; over 18 LAKH acres of patta land landed in the prohibited Part-B list. The Bhu Bharati Act 2025 exists to repair exactly that — restored 11-column Pahani, notices before amendments, a real appeal ladder, and a one-year correction window closing 13 April 2026.
  • This masterclass walks every column, every era, every ruling and every fraud — with an example for each. Budget 25–30 minutes. Worth it: these two documents decide every land deal you will ever do.

Part 1 — Why two records exist at all

Start with the question nobody asks: why does the government keep TWO separate books about the same field? Because the state historically cared about two different things. It cared about TAX — who must pay land revenue this year, on how much land, growing what, watered how — and that produced the Pahani, an annual working diary of each parcel, refreshed every agricultural year. And it cared about RIGHTS — who holds the parcel, how they got it, what claims sit on it — and that produced the Record of Rights, whose ownership extract is the 1-B. Tax needs freshness; rights need permanence. Two needs, two books.

Example: The shop analogy that makes it stick: the Pahani is the shop’s DAILY REGISTER — who opened the shutter today, what stock moved, who was behind the counter. The 1-B is the shop’s OWNERSHIP FILE — the partnership deed, who owns what share, which bank holds a charge. A stranger behind the counter for five years (an entry in the daily register) eventually becomes a legal problem for the ownership file — which is exactly what happens when a tenant sits in the Pahani’s cultivator column for years while the 1-B says someone else owns the land.

Hold that model through everything below: Pahani = the land’s life this year. 1-B = the land’s legal identity across years. Every fraud, every court case and every verification trick in this masterclass is some version of the two books disagreeing.

Part 2 — The 150-year story (why the old records still matter)

Telangana’s records did not begin with a website. Each era left a layer, and — this is the practical point — the OLDER layers outrank the newer ones when a correction case is fought, because they are closer to the original survey. Here is the whole lineage:

EraRecord createdWhat it is
1870s–1907Salar Jung’s revenue reforms → Land Revenue Act, 1317 FasliHyderabad State’s Diwan orders scientific measurement of all land; the 1907 Act mandates one sethwar and one village map per village, one tippan per survey number
Settlement eraSETHWAR (the “A” Register)The foundation record: every survey number’s extent, ownership, nature (patta/inam/government), soil class, irrigation source. Changes issue as SUPPLEMENTARY sethwars
Settlement eraTIPPAN (Field Measurement Book) + VILLAGE MAPThe tippan is the measured sketch of each survey field; the village map is the index that lets an officer find any field on the ground — the tools for boundary disputes and encroachment detection
1954-55KHASRA PAHANIThe great post-Nizam field census — the pahani of 1954-55 recorded pattadar, extent and pote kharab parcel by parcel. THE reference record for “what was true at the start”
1955–58SESALA PAHANIThree years of cultivation records across six crop seasons — crucially, it recorded PROTECTED TENANTS
CorrelationWASOOL BAQIThe bridge register linking OLD survey numbers to NEW ones village by village — how you trace a parcel across renumbering
1971RoR Act (AP/Telangana Rights in Land & Pattadar Pass Books Act)Modern Record of Rights: village-wise register of owners, pattadars, mortgagees, occupants; SECTION 6: every entry presumed true until contrary proved; periodic RoR exercises (1979-80, 1989-90)
1989PATTADAR PASSBOOK systemThe landmark: a physical book in the farmer’s hand combining the record extract with a title document — the direct ancestor of today’s Passbook-cum-Title Deed
2020RoR Act 2020 + DHARANI portalRegistration and mutation fused into one portal — but the record was THINNED, the enjoyment column dropped, and 18+ lakh acres of patta land fell into the prohibited Part-B list
2025BHU BHARATI (Record of Rights in Land) ActThe repair: restored 11-column pahani, Bhudhaar parcel IDs, notice-before-amendment, a four-rung appeal ladder, Land Tribunals, sada bainama regularisation, and the one-year correction window
Definition — Fasli year: the agricultural calendar the records run on, inherited from the Mughal revenue system. Convert roughly by adding 590: fasli 1425 ≈ 2015-16, fasli 1435 ≈ 2025-26. When an old pahani says “fasli 1364”, that is 1954-55 — the khasra pahani year.
Example: Why the lineage is not trivia: in a correction fight, the side holding the OLDER official paper usually wins. A Rangareddy family disputing a wrong Dharani-era name entry does not argue with screenshots — they produce the khasra pahani of 1954-55 showing the grandfather as pattadar, the sesala pahani showing the family cultivating, and the 1989 passbook. Three generations of state-issued paper against one migration error: the Tahsildar corrects the record. That is why this masterclass teaches the old records too — they are your ammunition, not history.
Example: And why the surveys matter: a CAG audit found that of the records the 1907 Act requires, only 4,712 sethwars and 7,392 village maps survived in the audited districts — the rest were MISSING, and no re-survey had been done in 60+ years. Every missing sethwar is a village where “what was originally true” is provable only with difficulty. When the drone resurvey and LPM numbers arrived (previous classes), they were fixing THIS.

Part 3 — The Pahani, column by column

The Pahani (also called Adangal, formally “Village Account No. 3”) is prepared by village revenue staff and maintained under the Tahsildar, updated every fasli. Dharani cut it to a stub; the Bhu Bharati Act restored the full multi-column format — the famous “11 columns”. Here is every column and what a professional reads in it:

ColumnWhat it recordsThe pro read
Survey number / sub-division (hissa)The parcel’s identity — e.g. 145/A, not just 145After family partitions, the PARENT number stops working on portals; search the sub-division. A deed citing 145 for land that is really 145/A/2 is sloppy drafting that will bite at registration
Khata numberThe holder’s ACCOUNT — groups all parcels one person holds in the villageOne khata, many survey numbers: the fastest way to see a seller’s full village holding
Pattadar name (+ father’s name)The recorded holderMust match the deed and Aadhaar to the SPELLING. Father’s name is your tiebreaker among namesakes
Anubhavadar / enjoyment (sagu)Who ACTUALLY occupies and cultivatesTHE column Dharani deleted and Bhu Bharati restored. Pattadar ≠ anubhavadar = tenancy or possession dispute until proven otherwise
Land naturePatta / Sarkari / Assigned / InamThe ten-second deal filter: Patta = proceed; Sarkari = government, stop; Assigned = welfare land, leave
ClassificationTari (wet) / Metta (dry) / Garden / Shikam / WasteSets honest value and legal use. Tari is irrigated and pricier; shikam is tank-bed land — a flooding and title minefield; waste may have no private ownership history at all
Water sourceCanal / tank / borewell / rain-fedCross-check against the classification: “wet” land with no water source listed is a classification worth challenging
Total extent vs cultivable extent + pote kharabThe whole parcel vs the usable partPote kharab is the unusable portion (rocks, paths, nala). A 5-00 acre parcel with 0-20 guntas pote kharab SELLS as 5 acres but FARMS as 4-20 — and conversion authorities treat the two portions differently
Soil class / water rateSettlement-era productivity gradingDrives old assessment and still surfaces in ceiling calculations (soil classes A–K decide how many acres one family may hold)
Crops by seasonWhat was grown each kharif/rabi, on how muchThe cultivation PROOF: 54B tax claims, crop loans and PMFBY insurance all lean on these entries. Empty crop columns on “farmland” = a record that will not support an agricultural-income story
Liabilities / encumbrancesLoans, charges, attachments recorded at revenue levelReads ALONGSIDE the EC — an MRO-level charge here may never appear in any EC search
Definition — Guntas: the unit under the acre: 1 acre = 40 guntas; 1 gunta = 121 square yards. A holding written “3-25” means 3 acres 25 guntas = 3.625 acres. Learn to read the dash — half the extent mismatches amateurs “find” are just misread guntas.
Example: Worked read #1 — the clean parcel: Survey 88/2, khata 214, pattadar K. Narsimha s/o Ramulu, anubhavadar K. Narsimha (same man), nature Patta, classification Metta (dry), source borewell, extent 2-00 / cultivable 1-38 (2 guntas pote kharab for a farm path), crops: cotton kharif fasli 1434, chillies rabi. Every column agrees with the story the seller told you. This is what boring — and buyable — looks like.
Example: Worked read #2 — the same parcel, poisoned: identical row, except anubhavadar reads “B. Yellaiah” and the crop columns show Yellaiah’s paddy for six consecutive faslis. Narsimha still owns it — but Yellaiah has six years of state-recorded cultivation, the raw material of a tenancy/possession claim (remember the sesala pahani’s protected tenants: under Section 38-E of the Tenancy Act 1950, long-recorded cultivators could be DEEMED OWNERS). Same owner, same land — the enjoyment column just moved this deal from “negotiate price” to “resolve occupant first, in writing, or walk”.
Example: Worked read #3 — the pote kharab surprise: a 10-00 acre “venture-ready” parcel shows cultivable 8-10. Where did 1-30 go? The column says nala (stream) + rocky outcrop. For a plotting developer that is not a footnote — the nala brings buffer-zone rules and layout dead space. The Pahani told you the site plan before the surveyor did.

Part 4 — The RoR 1-B, and the presumption that powers it

The 1-B is the ownership extract of the Record of Rights: pattadar, father’s name, khata, survey numbers, extent, MODE OF ACQUISITION (purchase / inheritance / partition / government assignment), and conditions or liabilities attached. Under the Bhu Bharati Act — as under the 1971 Act before it — the state must keep it current, and a separate RoR is now also prepared for ABADI (village-site) and non-agricultural lands, stored electronically with certified copies available to anyone, including banks, on payment of a fee.

Its legal engine is the PRESUMPTION OF TRUTH: every entry is presumed correct until the contrary is proved or the entry is lawfully amended. That presumption is why banks lend against the 1-B, why the registration system keys on it — and why a WRONG entry is so dangerous: the burden of proof sits on the person fighting the record, not the person enjoying it.

Definition — Mode of acquisition: the 1-B’s one-word biography of the holding: how the current holder got it. “Purchase” points you to a deed to verify; “inheritance” points to a succession chain (all heirs? married daughters?); “government assignment” ends the conversation — that land cannot be sold to you.
Example: The presumption, felt from both sides. FOR you: when your name is entered after clean mutation, anyone challenging your holding must PROVE you wrong — you start every dispute a goal up. AGAINST you: when a Dharani migration error spelled the owner’s name as someone else’s, the record’s presumption works for the WRONG name until corrected — which is precisely why the Act’s correction window (Part 7) exists, and why sleeping on a known error is legal self-harm.
Example: The auto-update rule as a lie detector, worked: seller says “I bought this in 2019.” In Telangana, registration of a sale flows into the record — so the 1-B should show him from 2019. You pull it: pattadar is still the previous owner, mode of acquisition still “inheritance” from HIS father. Two possibilities only: the 2019 “purchase” was never registered (a sada bainama — see Part 7), or it does not exist. Either way, the state’s own register just cross-examined your seller in thirty seconds, free.

Part 5 — Reading the two together: the disagreement playbook

Professionals never read one record. They pull both, same day, and read the GAPS. Every gap has a canonical meaning:

PatternMost likely storyYour move
1-B owner = seller; Pahani anubhavadar = someone elseTenant / adverse occupant in possessionResolve the occupant legally BEFORE price talk; long cultivation builds claims
Pahani shows seller; 1-B shows older nameMutation never completed on some linkSeller completes mutation first; never buy a promise to fix it later
Both show a DEAD manSuccession never filedLegal-heir certificate + succession mutation at the Tahsildar before anything; count the married daughters
Extents differ between the two recordsData-entry slip or an unrecorded partitionRoR correction with old pahanis as proof — before the window closes
1-B says Patta; Pahani land-nature says Assigned/SarkariA tampered or mistaken entry somewhereThe restrictive reading wins until the record is formally corrected: treat as restricted, investigate at the MRO
Crop columns empty for years on “farmed” landNobody actually cultivatesFine for a plotting purchase — fatal for 54B/agricultural-income claims that need a farming record
Example: Full worked example — one parcel, end to end. Target: 4-20 acres, Survey 172/B, near Shadnagar, quoted ₹1.1 crore/acre. Day 1, desk: 1-B — pattadar G. Lakshmamma w/o Malla Reddy, khata 88, mode: inheritance, extent 4-20, no liabilities. Pahani — anubhavadar G. Lakshmamma, nature Patta, classification Metta, borewell, cultivable 4-05 (15 guntas pote kharab: rocky), crops cotton for five faslis, liabilities: NIL. The two records AGREE. Day 2, ground: KYP at parcel centre matches 172/B; the woman in the fields is Lakshmamma’s daughter-in-law — consistent with family cultivation. Day 3, checks: 22A clean both portals, EC 30 years shows one released mortgage (2011 crop loan, discharged 2014 — matches a liabilities entry that later cleared). Diligence verdict: records coherent, price negotiable, the only file item is a succession consent from Lakshmamma’s children since mode = inheritance. THAT is what reading both records buys you: you now know the questions before the seller knows you have them.

Part 6 — What the courts actually say (case by case, in plain words)

This is where most buyers’ understanding is exactly backwards, so read slowly: the revenue record is strong evidence and weak title. The rulings, one by one:

Ande Gagaiah v. M. Krishna Reddy (Telangana HC): entries in revenue records neither confer rights nor extinguish the rights of a true owner. Meaning: getting your name into the pahani does not MAKE you owner; a wrong entry does not UNMAKE the real one.

Union of India v. Pramod Gupta (Supreme Court): no title can be claimed from a revenue entry — whoever claims ownership must prove the ownership was validly CONVEYED to them. Meaning: the entry is the shadow; the deed/grant is the object casting it.

State of Haryana v. Amin Lal (Supreme Court): revenue entries do not confer title, BUT they are admissible evidence of possession and can support an ownership claim alongside other evidence. Meaning: the record is a witness — a good one — never the judgment.

Sri Venkateshwara Developers v. Arepally Jeevan Rao (on Dharani itself): pahani entries on the portal are “not independently sustainable in law” — the portal is an administrative tool; courts decide on pleadings and instruments. Meaning: a Dharani/Bhu Bharati screenshot is not a trump card in litigation.

V. Ramaswamy v. State of Telangana (Section 38-E, Tenancy Act 1950): the Tahsildar can vest OWNERSHIP in a protected tenant and restore possession — the one great exception where record-plus-statute genuinely creates title, which is why old sesala pahani tenant entries still matter.

Definition — The three-layer model to keep: TITLE comes from instruments (registered deeds, grants, decrees, statutory vesting). RECORDS mirror title for administration and carry a rebuttable presumption. POSSESSION is fact on the ground, which records help prove. Confusing the layers is how buyers lose: they buy a record when they think they bought a title.
Example: The layers in action: a man holds a 2005 registered sale deed (title layer) but never mutated; the pahani still names the seller (record layer); and the seller’s nephew has quietly farmed the parcel for 12 years (possession layer). Who wins what? The deed-holder likely wins TITLE — but fights an adverse-possession claim built on the record’s cultivation entries, and until mutation cannot cleanly sell or borrow. Three layers, three different fights. A pro keeps all three aligned at all times; that alignment is what “clean” means.

Part 7 — Dharani’s wound and Bhu Bharati’s repair (the law you live under now)

What went wrong, 2020–2024: the RoR Act 2020 fused registration and mutation into Dharani — genuinely fast — but the record was cut to a stub: the enjoyment column REMOVED, detail columns dropped, and mass migration errors put patta lands into “Part-B”, the blocked list, which swelled past 18 LAKH acres. Grievances had nowhere to go but civil courts, because the 2020 framework never even got implementing rules. Landowners could see their land vanish from the record and had no ladder to climb.

What the 2025 Act does about it (passed December 2024; in force with Rules — GO Ms 39 — from 14 April 2025):

RepairThe mechanicsWhy you care
Restored record11-column Pahani back, enjoyment column back; separate RoR for abadi/non-agricultural land; village accounts (pahani, government land register, transfer register, irrigation register) maintained and filed yearlyThe record can again SHOW the disputes it used to hide
Notice before amendmentThe Tahsildar must issue written notice to every affected person, publish it, hear objections, THEN amendThe Dharani-era horror — waking up to a changed record — is now procedurally illegal
The appeal ladderTahsildar/Sub-Registrar order → RDO (60 days) → Collector (2nd appeal, 30 days) → LAND TRIBUNAL (final). Corrections on land worth over ₹5 lakh sit with the Collector directlyDisputes climb an administrative ladder in weeks instead of civil courts in decades; free legal aid for farmers who cannot afford appeals
Correction windowErrors since 2020 correctable on application (≈₹1,000 fee) until 13 APRIL 2026; RDO-level enquiries with village evidence, orders targeted in 90 daysOne clean year to fix the migration’s damage — after which entries harden toward finality
Sada bainama regularisationOld unregistered plain-paper sales (pre-2 June 2014) can be regularised on ground-level enquiryLakhs of informal family-era transactions get a legal path into the record
Succession disciplineFamily declaration + mandatory notices to ALL members before hereditary mutation; time-boundThe one-heir-grabs-all mutation trick is structurally blocked
Revision power (s.16)The Commissioner can reopen records of government, endowment, wakf, bhoodan and assigned lands changed FRAUDULENTLYFraudulent entries on protected categories are never safe, however old
Bhudhaar spineEvery parcel auto-assigned its GPS-anchored unique ID; passbook-cum-title-deed issued against the recordThe identity system the previous classes covered — this Act is where it is born
Example: The ladder, worked: a Siddipet farmer finds his 3-10 acres recorded as 2-10 after migration. Old path (2021): a civil suit, ₹50,000+ in fees, five-plus years. New path: correction application with the 1989 passbook and khasra pahani attached (~₹1,000) → Tahsildar/RDO enquiry with neighbours → order targeted inside 90 days; if refused, RDO appeal within 60 days, then Collector, then Tribunal — each rung time-bound. The substance of his case is identical; the SYSTEM around it is transformed. That is what a records statute is actually for.
Example: The deadline, worked backwards: it is late August 2026 in this paper’s world — the 13 April 2026 window has CLOSED for casual corrections. What survives? Appeals up the ladder, the s.16 revision route for fraud on protected lands, and civil remedies. The lesson for every future window (and there will be others as the resurvey lands): the state occasionally opens a door that turns litigation into paperwork. Professionals walk through doors while they are open — the previous RoR-correction class exists because we watched this one close.

Part 8 — The fraud museum (real cases from the state’s own audit)

These are not hypotheticals. India’s national auditor (CAG) examined Telangana’s land records and published what it found — each finding is a masterclass in how record systems fail:

The 500 phantom acres (Shamshabad): the khasra pahani showed survey number 62 of Bahadurguda holding 500 acres of GOVERNMENT land. The tippan and village map showed SN 62 physically smaller than five neighbouring numbers totalling ~63 acres — and the Tahsildar confirmed the 500 acres did not physically exist. Government land worth ₹24.42 crore, unaccounted, because three records that should agree never did. Lesson: extent claims are checked against the MAP records (tippan/FMB), not just other text records.

The tampered khasra (SN 65): a survey number recorded as 547-27 acres was sub-divided into 33 parts; departmental enquiry found the 1954-55 khasra pahani itself had been TAMPERED with — wrong entries written into the foundation record — and title stayed unsettled decades later. Lesson: even the “root” record can be corrupted; multi-generation cross-checking (sethwar vs khasra vs current) is how tampering surfaces.

The reclassified government land (Alwal): 16 acres classified as maqtha (a government-linked category) in the sesala pahani appeared as PATTA land in the 2013-14 pahani — and occupants then claimed ownership from the newer entry. Lesson: a favourable NEW entry against an unfavourable OLD one is a red flag pointing backwards, not a windfall.

The drifting village (Rameshwarpally): between the sethwar and the pahani, 83 survey numbers showed 122 MORE acres in the pahani and 36 showed 14 FEWER. A whole village whose two core records disagree by over a hundred acres. Lesson: extent errors are systemic, not exotic — which is why resurveys and the LPM exist.

The half-done mutation (Cheeryal): five sons inherited; the mutation was carried into the RoR 1-B but never into the pahani for years. Lesson: mutation is not done when ONE record updates — it is done when BOTH do. Check both after every transaction, including your own.

Example: Turn the museum into a checklist: (1) map records against text records for extent; (2) oldest layer against newest for classification and nature; (3) both current records against each other after every mutation; (4) any too-good newer entry investigated backwards. Four habits, each born from a real failure above — this is what “learning from other people’s disasters” literally looks like.

Part 9 — Getting the records (the practical mechanics, complete)

Free online view: bhubharati.telangana.gov.in (agricultural; Dharani redirects here) or ccla.telangana.gov.in → Land Details Search → district, mandal, village → search by survey/sub-division number, khata, passbook number, or name (see the name-search class). Read on screen, download, print — research grade only. Certified copies (the legal grade): MeeSeva in the land’s own mandal — Pahani ₹35, RoR 1-B ₹25, about 15 minutes for current records; the Act guarantees certified extracts to ANY applicant including banks. Digitally signed portal copies carry QR + Bhudhaar (see the certified-copy class). Old records (khasra pahani, sesala pahani, old faslis): apply at the Tahsildar’s office — days, not minutes, and worth every day when a correction or dispute is live. The urban split: agricultural records live here; EC, market value and deed copies live on IGRS (registration.telangana.gov.in) — the two-portal rule from the hub class.

Example: The ₹95 full stack: certified Pahani (₹35) + certified 1-B (₹25) + a fresh signed portal PDF (~₹35 equivalent fee). For less than a hundred rupees and one MeeSeva visit you hold the complete, bank-grade, current truth about any parcel in Telangana. There is no cheaper professional edge in Indian real estate.

Part 10 — The glossary (speak the record’s language)

TermMeaning
Pahani / AdangalThe annual village cultivation-and-holding record (Village Account 3)
RoR 1-BThe ownership extract of the Record of Rights
PattadarThe recorded holder liable for land revenue
Anubhavadar / saguThe “enjoyer” — actual occupant/cultivator
KhataThe holder’s account grouping their parcels in a village
KhatadarThe person to whom the khata belongs
Tari / MettaWet (irrigated) / dry (rain-fed) land
ShikamTank-bed land — flood-prone, restricted, frequent litigation
PorambokeGovernment/common land — roads, riverbeds, grazing; never privately saleable
Pote kharabThe unusable portion inside a parcel (rocks, paths, nala)
Inam / maqtha / jagirNizam-era grant tenures, abolished post-1948 — legacy entries that still complicate titles
Kharij khataAn account struck off / held in abeyance — investigate before touching
Sada bainamaAn unregistered plain-paper sale — regularisable (pre-June-2014) under the new Act
FasliThe revenue year (≈ calendar year + 590)
JamabandiThe annual closing/audit of village accounts at fasli-end
Sethwar / tippan / wasool baqiSettlement register / field sketch / old-to-new survey correlation register
Example: Why vocabulary is leverage: at a Tahsildar counter, “the record is wrong” gets a shrug; “the pahani’s anubhavadar column contradicts the 1-B and the khasra pahani of fasli 1364 — I want a correction under the Act with these certified copies attached” gets a file opened. Officials triage by fluency. So do sellers.

Quick answers (FAQ)

Which one proves ownership? Neither, by itself — courts are unanimous. The 1-B carries a presumption; title lives in registered instruments. Keep deed + 1-B + Pahani aligned and the question never bites. How current is each? Pahani: every fasli (and on transactions); 1-B: on every registered transaction/mutation. Are Dharani-era entries trustworthy? Treat 2020–2025 entries as the error-prone layer — verify against pre-2020 paper where stakes are high. What are the exact fees? View free; certified Pahani ₹35, 1-B ₹25; corrections ≈ ₹1,000 (window now closed — appeals remain). Can I get someone else’s records? Yes — the Act makes certified extracts available to all applicants; land records are public by design. What single habit captures this whole masterclass? Pull BOTH records, same day, before every conversation about any parcel — and file dated copies every time.

Bottom line

Two books, one truth — when they agree. The Pahani tells you what the land IS this year; the 1-B tells you WHOSE it has been across years; the courts tell you neither is title but both are evidence; history tells you the older layers are your ammunition; and the new Act finally gives you a ladder when the books are wrong. Master these two documents and every other Telangana land record — EC, passbook, Bhudhaar, map — becomes a footnote to a story you already know how to read.

For you: Institutionalise this: (1) a per-parcel “record file” — dated certified Pahani + 1-B at acquisition, at every loan, at listing, and every jamabandi (fasli-end) as an annual audit of your whole land bank; (2) the disagreement playbook (Part 5) printed for your team — gaps between the two records ARE the due-diligence findings; (3) for JD sourcing, the khata trick — one khata search shows a family’s entire village holding before they choose what to tell you; (4) when any dispute stirs, go OLD first: khasra pahani, sesala, sethwar — the record generations that outrank whatever a portal shows today. The developer who reads both books faster than the market prices them owns the corridor. Original Deccan Ledger deep-research masterclass; sources below.
